


Smoothed Picard Hamiltonian Monte Carlo




 

  // Restore the saved color scheme preference, or
  // enact the browser preference if "automatic", 
  // without expecting DOM load to have completed.
  //
  // Also restore any saved readingmode and ToC display preferences.
  function initializeReadingPreferences() {
    let saved_theme = localStorage.getItem("ar5iv_theme") || "automatic";
    if (saved_theme === "automatic") {
      if (window.matchMedia("(prefers-color-scheme: dark)").matches) {
        saved_theme = "dark";
      }
    }
    if (saved_theme == "dark") {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-theme", "dark");
    } else {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-theme", "light");
    }

    const tocDisplay = localStorage.getItem('arxiv_html_paper_toc_display');
    if (tocDisplay) {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-toc-display", tocDisplay);
    }
    const readingMode = localStorage.getItem('arxiv_html_paper_reading_mode');
    if (readingMode) {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-reading-mode", readingMode);
    }
    // Pre-apply spinout-banner dismissal here, before the banner paints, so it
    // never flashes in only to be hidden later by the deferred arxiv-header.js.
    // Key matches arxiv-header.js: "arxiv-banner-dismissed:" + data-banner-name.
    if (localStorage.getItem('arxiv-banner-dismissed:spinout-nonprofit')) {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-banner-dismissed", "");
    }
  }
  // Run as soon as JS starts, to minimize repainting
  initializeReadingPreferences();

arXiv:2609.06906v1 [math.ST] 07 Sep 2026




Smoothed Picard Hamiltonian Monte Carlo



Fan Chen
††thanks: Department of Electrical Engineering and Computer Science,
Massachusetts Institute of Technology.
Email: fanchen@mit.edu.
  
Sinho Chewi
††thanks: Department of Statistics and Data Science, Yale University.
Email: sinho.chewi@yale.edu.
  
Jianfeng Lu
††thanks: Department of Mathematics, Duke University.
Email: jianfeng@math.duke.edu.
  
Matthew S. Zhang
††thanks: Department of Mathematics, Massachusetts Institute of Technology.
Email: shuns436@mit.edu.

August 2026


@abstract1 Abstract
    

@abstract1.1 We develop a new low-accuracy sampler, called smoothed Picard Hamiltonian Monte Carlo, which combines Gaussian smoothing, Picard iteration, and higher-order discretization.
For a log-concave target  [\pi\propto\exp(-V)]  in dimension  [d]  satisfying  [0\prec\alpha I\preceq\nabla^{2}V\preceq\beta I] , with condition number  [\kappa\coloneqq\beta/\alpha] , smoothed Picard HMC returns a sample with  [\sqrt{\alpha}\,W_{2}(\cdot,\pi)\leqslant\varepsilon]  using  [\widetilde{O}(\kappa^{2}+\kappa^{7/6}d^{1/6}/\varepsilon^{1/3})]  gradient queries.
We also prove stronger  [W_{q}]  bounds, and then develop an algorithmic framework, the recursive warm start generator, to upgrade these  [W_{q}]  bounds to stronger divergence guarantees.
This produces a warm start for the proximal bouncy particle sampler, introduced in a companion work, leading to a high-accuracy log-concave sampler with complexity  [\widetilde{O}((\kappa^{7/6}d^{1/6}+\kappa^{1/2}d^{1/4})\operatorname{polylog}(1/\varepsilon))] .

@S1 

1 Introduction


@S1.p1 

@S1.p1.1 We study the problem of sampling from a target probability density  [\pi\propto\exp(-V)]  over  [\mathbb{R}^{d}] , where
 [V\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})]  satisfies, for  [0<\alpha\leqslant\beta] ,





 [\alpha I\preceq\nabla^{2}V(x)\preceq\beta I\,,\qquad x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,,\qquad\kappa\coloneqq\frac{\beta}{\alpha}\,.] 

(1.1)


@S1.p1.2 We design a new sampler that combines Gaussian smoothing of the density with a high-order
discretization of Hamiltonian dynamics via Picard iteration and Chebyshev–Lobatto quadrature. This combination gives a stable unadjusted Hamiltonian step even
though the original potential is assumed to have only two derivatives.


@S1.p2 

@S1.p2.1 Our first result is a low-accuracy sampler with Wasserstein guarantee.


@S1.Thmtheorem1 
Theorem 1.1 ( [W_{2}]  guarantee).

@S1.Thmtheorem1.p1 

@S1.Thmtheorem1.p1.1 Assume (1.1), let  [0<\varepsilon\leqslant 1] , and suppose that we are given a point
 [x_{\rm ref}]  with
 [\lVert\nabla V(x_{\rm ref})\rVert\leqslant\sqrt{\alpha d}] .
Then, the smoothed Picard HMC algorithm
(Algorithm 3.2) returns a sample from a probability law  [\widehat{\pi}]  satisfying





 [\sqrt{\alpha}\,W_{2}(\widehat{\pi},\pi)\leqslant\varepsilon\,.] 



@S1.Thmtheorem1.p1.2 The expected number of gradient and proximal queries is at most





 [O\Bigl(\Bigl\{\kappa^{2}+\frac{\kappa^{7/6}d^{1/6}}{\varepsilon^{1/3}}\Bigr\}\log^{4}\frac{e\kappa d}{\varepsilon}\Bigr)\,.] 





@S1.p3 

@S1.p3.1 We find the dimension dependence in this result to be quite surprising.
Prior to this work, under (1.1), the best dimension dependence was  [d^{1/3}] .
This was achieved with the randomized midpoint discretization of the
underdamped Langevin, with rate
 [\widetilde{O}(\kappa d^{1/3}/\varepsilon^{2/3})]  in  [W_{2}]  [55], and also the shifted ODE method [29].
With the development of the shifted composition framework [1] and accelerated entropic hypocoercivity [41, 39], the rate was improved to  [\widetilde{O}(\kappa^{5/6}d^{1/3}/\varepsilon^{2/3})]  in  [\KL] . The closely related Poisson
midpoint discretization of underdamped Langevin achieved an incomparable rate of roughly  [\widetilde{O}(\kappa^{7/6}d^{1/3}/\varepsilon^{1/3}+\kappa^{11/8}d^{3/8}/\varepsilon^{1/4})]  in  [W_{2}]  [59]. More recently, exact simulation of the underdamped Langevin
diffusion via FORS achieved
 [\widetilde{O}(\kappa^{2/3}d^{1/3}\operatorname{polylog}(1/\varepsilon))]  complexity
[17].
In contrast, Theorem 1.1 achieves dimension dependence  [d^{1/6}] .


@S1.p4 

@S1.p4.1 In a companion work [16], we introduce a new sampler, called the proximal bouncy particle sampler or proximal BPS, with  [\widetilde{O}(\kappa^{1/2}d^{1/4}\operatorname{polylog}(1/\varepsilon))]  complexity from a warm start. One key application of smoothed Picard HMC is to provide an implementable warm start for that sampler.


@S1.p5 

@S1.p5.1 Toward this end, we develop a novel algorithmic framework which we call the recursive warm start generator. It is designed as a wrapper around smoothed samplers—that is, samplers which target the Gaussian smoothed distribution  [\pi*\mathsf{N}(0,\eta I)] —and it upgrades transport metric guarantees into stronger divergence guarantees. Namely, with negligible overhead, it upgrades  [W_{2}]  bounds into  [\KL]  bounds;  [W_{q}]  bounds into mixed TV and Rényi bounds; and  [W_{\psi_{2}}]  bounds into genuine Rényi bounds. We apply this to smoothed Picard HMC by extending the arguments for Theorem 1.1 to produce stronger  [W_{q}]  guarantees, leading to the following warm start result.


@S1.Thmtheorem2 
Theorem 1.2 (Warm start).

@S1.Thmtheorem2.p1 

@S1.Thmtheorem2.p1.1 Assume (1.1) and that we are given  [x_{\rm ref}]  satisfying
 [\lVert\nabla V(x_{\rm ref})\rVert\leqslant\sqrt{\alpha d}] . For every
 [0<\delta<1/2] , the algorithm of
Section 3.4 returns a sample from a law
 [\widehat{\pi}_{\delta}]  with the following guarantee.
There exists a law  [\widehat{\pi}_{\delta}^{\dagger}]  satisfying





 [\mathsf{TV}(\widehat{\pi}_{\delta},\widehat{\pi}_{\delta}^{\dagger})\leqslant\delta\,,\qquad\mathsf{R}_{2}(\widehat{\pi}_{\delta}^{\dagger}\mathbin{\|}\pi)\leqslant 1\,.] 



@S1.Thmtheorem2.p1.2 Moreover, the expected number of gradient queries is at most





 [O\Bigl(\kappa^{7/6}d^{1/6}\log^{9}\frac{e\kappa d}{\delta}\Bigr)\,.] 





@S1.p6 

@S1.p6.1 In Theorem 7.1, we prove a stronger statement that relaxes the assumption of strong log-concavity to a log-Sobolev inequality, and provides a KL divergence guarantee.
Note that the recursive warm start generator also removes the  [\kappa^{2}]  term from the complexity.


@S1.p7 

@S1.p7.1 Finally, by using this warm start result to initialize proximal BPS [16], we obtain a new high-accuracy sampler which enjoys the following guarantee.


@S1.Thmtheorem3 
Theorem 1.3 (Warm start + proximal BPS).

@S1.Thmtheorem3.p1 

@S1.Thmtheorem3.p1.1 Assume (1.1) and suppose that we are given a point  [x_{\rm ref}]  with
 [\lVert\nabla V(x_{\rm ref})\rVert\leqslant\sqrt{\alpha d}] . For every
 [0<\varepsilon<1/4] , the warm start
algorithm followed by the proximal BPS algorithm returns a sample from a law
 [\widehat{\pi}]  satisfying





 [\mathsf{TV}(\widehat{\pi},\pi)\leqslant\varepsilon\,.] 



@S1.Thmtheorem3.p1.2 The expected number of gradient evaluations is





 [O\Bigl(\kappa^{7/6}d^{1/6}\log^{9}\frac{e\kappa d}{\varepsilon}+\kappa^{1/2}d^{1/4}\log^{11/4}\frac{e\kappa d}{\varepsilon}\Bigr)\,.] 





@S1.p8 

@S1.p8.1 Prior to this work, the best high-accuracy sampler was exact diffusion simulation [17], which achieved a complexity of  [\widetilde{O}(\kappa^{2/3}d^{1/3}\operatorname{polylog}(1/\varepsilon))] . Hence, our new high-accuracy sampler has state-of-the-art dependence on the dimension, but not on the condition number.


@S1.Thmtheorem4 
Remark 1.4 (Further consequences).

@S1.Thmtheorem4.p1 

@S1.Thmtheorem4.p1.1 The proximal sampler reduction [37, 19] converts general
log-smooth sampling problems into well-conditioned log-concave subproblems.
By invoking this framework, we could also state corollaries in other settings, such as when  [\pi]  is only assumed to satisfy a Poincaré inequality.
Since this reduction is standard, we omit these extensions for brevity.



@S1.p9 

@S1.p9.1 We give an overview of the main new ideas—both algorithmic and analytic—introduced in this work in the technical overview (Section 3).


@S1.SS0.SSS0.Px1 
Related work.


@S1.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1 

@S1.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1.1 This work advances the study of the complexity of log-concave sampling; an
introduction to the subject and an overview of existing approaches can be
found in [24].


@S1.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p2 

@S1.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p2.1 Our use of Gaussian smoothing is reminiscent of score-based diffusion
models [56, 57, 31, 58] and stochastic localization [27],
which also rely on score functions for Gaussian convolutions of a target distribution.
Here, we do not learn score
functions from data: we use queries to the unsmoothed score to construct
nearly unbiased estimators of the smoothed score, and control their bias
and fluctuations in the sampling analysis.


@S1.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p3 

@S1.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p3.1 Hamiltonian Monte Carlo was introduced as hybrid Monte Carlo in lattice
field theory [26]; see [49] for its development in statistics.
Early analyses quantified its favorable dimension dependence through
asymptotic scaling results for product targets [8].
Subsequent works established non-asymptotic convergence guarantees for
ideal HMC [38, 9, 44, 22].
More recent work has focused on accelerated convergence of randomized
HMC and related piecewise deterministic processes, including convergence
in relative entropy [40, 47, 48].


@S1.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p4 

@S1.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p4.1 A substantial literature studies numerical discretization, both for
unadjusted HMC [45, 43, 10, 11, 30, 12, 64]
and for Metropolized HMC [20, 36, 21, 64].
Representative results achieve  [d^{1/4}]  dimension dependence under
quantitative third derivative bounds, such as structured or
Frobenius-Lipschitz Hessian assumptions [45, 21, 64].
Higher-order integrators and polynomial collocation yield still better
dimension dependence under stronger smoothness and structural
assumptions [42, 38].
Our departure is to improve the dimension dependence without imposing
higher-order smoothness on the original potential: Gaussian smoothing
provides the regularity needed for accurate integration, while we control
the error from estimating the smoothed score using queries to the
unsmoothed score.


@S1.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p5 

@S1.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p5.1 The recursive warm start framework builds heavily on the proximal
sampler [37, 19].
The main analytic mechanism for warm start generation is regularization
along the heat flow, leading to reverse transport inequalities. Such inequalities are
dual formulations of dimension-free Harnack inequalities, originating in
Wang’s work [62, 63]; see also
[7, 3] for the transport formulation.
Related regularization arguments have been used to generate algorithmic
warm starts with underdamped Langevin [2] and HMC [64],
and to establish KL divergence guarantees via the shifted composition
framework [4, 1].
Coupling-based regularization has also yielded total variation
bounds [10] and divergence guarantees for unadjusted
HMC [12]. These works analyze regularization for particular
sampling dynamics. Our framework instead applies heat flow regularization
to the output of any smoothed sampler, yielding a general route to KL, mixed TV and Rényi, or genuine
Rényi warm starts.



@S1.SS0.SSS0.Px2 
AI usage.


@S1.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1 

@S1.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1.1 The main ideas underlying the algorithm and its analysis emerged through extensive interactions with GPT-5.6 Sol. The initial goal was to improve the dimensional dependence beyond  [d^{1/3}] . In response, GPT proposed considering the smoothed potential  [\widehat{V}_{\eta}\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{G\sim\mathsf{N}(0,I)}V(\cdot+\sqrt{\eta}\,G)] , which led to a sampler with dimensional dependence  [d^{1/4}] . We subsequently suggested the more natural smoothing adopted in this paper and, through further refinements of the analysis and extensive interactions between the authors and GPT, obtained the improved dimensional dependence and the algorithms presented here. The authors independently checked the arguments, prepared the manuscript, and take full responsibility for its contents.




@S2 

2 Preliminaries


@S2.p1 

@S2.p1.1 Except in the generic warm start result Theorem 6.1
and its consequence Theorem 7.1, throughout the remainder of the paper
 [V\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})]  and (1.1) holds pointwise.
For most of the paper, we normalize  [\beta=1] , so that  [\kappa^{-1}I\preceq\nabla^{2}V\preceq I] ; this can be achieved by rescaling.


@S2.p2 

@S2.p2.1 For simplicity of exposition, we initially allow ourselves access to proximal queries to  [V] , in addition to queries to  [\nabla V] .
For step size  [a>0] , one proximal query returns





 [\operatorname{prox}_{aV}(y)\coloneqq\operatorname*{argmin}_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\Bigl\{V(x)+\frac{\lVert x-y\rVert^{2}}{2a}\Bigr\}}\,.] 

(2.1)


@S2.p2.2 In this paper, we only ever need to call the proximal oracle for a  [\beta] -smooth potential with step size at most  [1/(2\beta)] .
In this regime, the computation of  [\operatorname{prox}_{aV}]  is a well-conditioned optimization problem and can be easily implemented via standard routines.
In Appendix D, we remove the use of proximal queries altogether.


@S2.p3 

@S2.p3.1 The sampling analogue of the proximal operator is the restricted Gaussian oracle (RGO), which is the distribution





 [R_{a,y}(\mathrm{d}x)\propto\exp\Bigl\{-V(x)-\frac{\lVert x-y\rVert^{2}}{2a}\Bigr\}\,\mathrm{d}x\,.] 



@S2.p3.2 In Appendix A, we show how to approximately implement it in  [\widetilde{O}(1+{(a^{2}d)}^{1/3})]  queries, based on the exact diffusion sampler of [17].


@S2.p4 

@S2.p4.1 We assume that an admissible reference point is supplied:





 [\lVert\nabla V(x_{\rm ref})\rVert\leqslant\sqrt{\alpha d}\,.] 

(2.2)


@S2.p4.2 This reference point could be obtained via an optimization routine.


@S2.p5 

@S2.p5.1 The total variation distance is
 [\mathsf{TV}(P,Q)\coloneqq\sup_{A}|P(A)-Q(A)|] .
We also use





 [\KL(P\mathbin{\|}Q)\coloneqq\int\log\bigl(\frac{\mathrm{d}P}{\mathrm{d}Q}\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}P\,,\qquad W_{2}^{2}(P,Q)\coloneqq\inf_{(X,Y)}\mathbb{E}\lVert X-Y\rVert^{2}\,,] 



@S2.p5.2 with the usual value  [+\infty]  for KL without absolute continuity; the
infimum in the definition of  [W_{2}]  is over all couplings of  [P]  and  [Q] .
For  [q>1] , define Rényi divergence by





 [\mathsf{R}_{q}(P\mathbin{\|}Q)\coloneqq\frac{1}{q-1}\log\int\bigl(\frac{\mathrm{d}P}{\mathrm{d}Q}\bigr)^{q}\,\mathrm{d}Q\,,] 



@S2.p5.3 with value  [+\infty]  when  [P\not\ll Q] ; this is the standard convention
of [61].


@S2.p6 

@S2.p6.1 For matrices and tensors,  [\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}]  and
 [\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}]  denote the operator and Hilbert–Schmidt (or Frobenius) norms,
respectively. For an  [r] -tensor  [A]  on  [\mathbb{R}^{d}] , this means





 [\lVert A\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^{2}=\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{r}\in[d]}\bigl|A[e_{j_{1}},\ldots,e_{j_{r}}]\bigr|^{2}\,,] 



@S2.p6.2 where  [(e_{j})_{j\in[d]}]  is any orthonormal basis of  [\mathbb{R}^{d}] .


@S2.p7 

@S2.p7.1 The notation  [\widetilde{O}(B)]  means  [B]  times a fixed universal power of a
logarithm of the dimension, condition number, inverse accuracy, and any
explicit confidence parameter. Throughout,  [C,c>0]  denote universal
constants that may change from line to line.



@S3 

3 Technical overview


@S3.p1 

@S3.p1.1 Recall our convention that  [\beta=1] , so that  [\kappa^{-1}I\preceq\nabla^{2}V\preceq I] .


@S3.SS1 

3.1 The smoothing trick


@S3.SS1.p1 

@S3.SS1.p1.1 For a smoothing variance
 [\eta>0] , write





 [\pi_{\eta}\coloneqq\pi*\mathsf{N}(0,\eta I)\,,\qquad\Pi_{\eta}\coloneqq\pi_{\eta}\otimes\mathsf{N}(0,I)\,.] 



@S3.SS1.p1.2 The key algorithmic idea of this paper is to pick a smoothing parameter
 [\eta>0]  and to sample from the smoothed distribution  [\pi_{\eta}] 
instead of the original target  [\pi] . If
 [\law(Y)\approx\pi_{\eta}] , then we can recover a sample from  [\pi]  via
the RGO: for  [X\sim R_{\eta,Y}] , we have  [\law(X)\approx\pi] .


@S3.SS1.p2 

@S3.SS1.p2.1 The smoothed
potential  [V_{\eta}] , defined by
 [e^{-V_{\eta}}\coloneqq e^{-V}*\mathsf{N}(0,\eta I)] , is given by





 [\nabla V_{\eta}(y)=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\nabla V(X)\bigm|X+\sqrt{\eta}\,G=y\bigr]\,,] 

(3.1)


@S3.SS1.p2.2 where  [G\sim\mathsf{N}(0,I)]  and  [X\sim\pi]  are independent.
The smoothed potential admits quantitative higher-order derivative bounds, allowing us to apply higher-order integrators.
The catch is that (3.1) is given as a conditional expectation, and is therefore not explicitly computable.
Write  [g_{\eta}\coloneqq\nabla V_{\eta}] .


@S3.SS1.p3 

@S3.SS1.p3.1 Therefore, we replace evaluations of  [\nabla V_{\eta}]  with





 [\widehat{g}_{\eta}(y;G)\coloneqq\nabla V\bigl(\operatorname{prox}_{\eta V}(y)+\sqrt{\eta}\,G\bigr)\,,\qquad G\sim\mathsf{N}(0,I)\,.] 

(3.2)


@S3.SS1.p3.2 The intuition is that
 [\nabla V_{\eta}(y)=\int\nabla V\,\mathrm{d}R_{\eta,y}] , and
 [\law(\operatorname{prox}_{\eta V}(y)+\sqrt{\eta}\,G)\approx R_{\eta,y}] , so that
 [\widehat{g}_{\eta}(y;G)]  is almost an unbiased estimator of
 [\nabla V_{\eta}(y)] .



@S3.SS2 

3.2 The higher-order integrator and the  [W_{2}]  guarantee


@S3.SS2.SSS0.Px1 
Picard iteration.


@S3.SS2.SSS0.Px1.p1 

@S3.SS2.SSS0.Px1.p1.1 The numerical scheme we consider below is a discretization of an unadjusted generalized Hamiltonian sampler
with partial Ornstein–Uhlenbeck (OU) momentum refreshment, following the terminology
of [30].
Between momentum refreshments, we follow the Hamiltonian dynamics





 [\displaystyle\dot{X}_{t}=P_{t}\,,\qquad\dot{P}_{t}=-\nabla V_{\eta}(X_{t})\,.] 



@S3.SS2.SSS0.Px1.p1.2 The dynamics can be written in integral form as





 [X_{t}=X_{0}+tP_{0}-\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)\,g_{\eta}(X_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s\,,\qquad P_{t}=P_{0}-\int_{0}^{t}g_{\eta}(X_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s\,.] 

(3.3)



@S3.SS2.SSS0.Px1.p2 

@S3.SS2.SSS0.Px1.p2.1 Since the integral cannot generally be computed explicitly, we simulate it with Picard iteration. Let  [{(X_{t}^{[0]},P_{t}^{[0]})}_{t\geqslant 0}]  be a computable initial guess for the trajectory.
The ideal Picard iteration maps this to an updated trajectory  [{(X_{t}^{[1]},P_{t}^{[1]})}_{t\geqslant 0}]  by applying (3.3) but with the non-linear term  [g_{\eta}]  evaluated at the preceding trajectory.
That is,





 [\displaystyle X_{t}^{[1]}\coloneqq X_{0}+tP_{0}-\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)\,g_{\eta}(X_{s}^{[0]})\,\mathrm{d}s\,,\qquad P_{t}^{[1]}\coloneqq P_{0}-\int_{0}^{t}g_{\eta}(X_{s}^{[0]})\,\mathrm{d}s\,.] 

(3.4)


@S3.SS2.SSS0.Px1.p2.2 A particularly simple choice for the initial trajectory is
 [X_{t}^{[0]}\coloneqq X_{0}+tP_{0}] .


@S3.SS2.SSS0.Px1.p3 

@S3.SS2.SSS0.Px1.p3.1 This describes one Picard iteration, and in principle one can iterate further.
For the main results of this paper, it suffices to iterate up to depth  [2] .
Higher depth iteration is treated in Appendix E.


@S3.SS2.SSS0.Px1.p4 

@S3.SS2.SSS0.Px1.p4.1 The ideal Picard iteration requires exact integration, which is not directly implementable.
To develop an algorithm, we apply numerical integration, or quadrature.



@S3.SS2.SSS0.Px2 
Chebyshev–Lobatto quadrature.


@S3.SS2.SSS0.Px2.p1 

@S3.SS2.SSS0.Px2.p1.1 Fix an integer  [J\geqslant 2] . The Chebyshev–Lobatto nodes on the interval  [[0,h]]  are defined to be





 [t_{j}\coloneqq\frac{h}{2}\,\bigl\{1-\cos\bigl({\textstyle\frac{j-1}{J-1}}\,\uppi\bigr)\bigr\}\,,\qquad j=1,\ldots,J\,.] 



@S3.SS2.SSS0.Px2.p1.2 Associated with the nodes are certain interpolating polynomials  [\ell_{j}] ,  [j=1,\dotsc,J] ,
characterized by
 [\ell_{j}(t_{i})=\mathbf{1}_{\{i=j\}}] . Then, for every vector-valued function
 [f]  defined at the nodes, let





 [(\mathcal{I}_{J,h}f)(t)\coloneqq\sum_{j=1}^{J}f(t_{j})\,\ell_{j}(t)] 

(3.5)


@S3.SS2.SSS0.Px2.p1.3 be its Lagrange interpolant; this is an approximation to  [f(t)]  itself.
Background material on Chebyshev–Lobatto quadrature is collected in Appendix B.


@S3.SS2.SSS0.Px2.p2 

@S3.SS2.SSS0.Px2.p2.1 To obtain a numerical scheme, we replace  [g_{\eta}]  in (3.4) with its interpolant.
This produces the iterations





 [\displaystyle X_{t}^{[1]}] 
 [\displaystyle=X_{0}+tP_{0}-\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)\,\bigl(\mathcal{I}_{J,h}[g_{\eta}\circ X^{[0]}]\bigr)(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\,,] 
 [\displaystyle P_{t}^{[1]}] 
 [\displaystyle=P_{0}-\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{I}_{J,h}[g_{\eta}\circ X^{[0]}](s)\,\mathrm{d}s\,,] 




 [\displaystyle X_{t}^{[2]}] 
 [\displaystyle=X_{0}+tP_{0}-\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)\,\bigl(\mathcal{I}_{J,h}[g_{\eta}\circ X^{[1]}]\bigr)(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\,,] 
 [\displaystyle P_{t}^{[2]}] 
 [\displaystyle=P_{0}-\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{I}_{J,h}[g_{\eta}\circ X^{[1]}](s)\,\mathrm{d}s\,.] 



@S3.SS2.SSS0.Px2.p2.2 Note that we only ever need to evaluate these paths at the nodes  [t_{j}] .
We can write the iterations in terms of the precomputed quantities





 [\displaystyle\omega_{j}] 
 [\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{0}^{h}\ell_{j}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\,,] 
 [\displaystyle\omega_{i,j}] 
 [\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{0}^{t_{i}}(t_{i}-t)\,\ell_{j}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\,,] 
 [\displaystyle 1\leqslant i,j\leqslant J\,.] 

(3.6)


@S3.SS2.SSS0.Px2.p2.3 Finally, we replace  [g_{\eta}]  by the stochastic-gradient approximation
 [\widehat{g}_{\eta}]  from (3.2). Thus, throughout
this subsection, the algorithm has exact access to  [\operatorname{prox}_{\eta V}] .
We replace these proximal queries by gradient queries in
Appendix D.


@S3.SS2.SSS0.Px2.p3 

@S3.SS2.SSS0.Px2.p3.1 This leads to the following algorithm.


@S3.Thmtheorem1 
Algorithm 3.1 (Smoothed Picard HMC).

@S3.Thmtheorem1.p1 

@S3.Thmtheorem1.p1.1 Initialize
 [X_{\rm init}\coloneqq x_{\rm ref}]  and
 [P_{\rm init}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,I)] . Given
 [(X_{\rm init},P_{\rm init})] , one
phase draws independent standard Gaussians
 [\zeta_{0},\zeta_{1},(G_{j}^{[0]},G_{j}^{[1]})_{j=1}^{J}] , and performs the following steps.

1. 

@S3.I1.i1.p1 

@S3.I1.i1.p1.1 Partially refresh the momentum and create the starting trajectory.


@S3.I1.i1.p2 

@S3.I1.i1.p2.1 Set  [P_{0}\coloneqq e^{-h/2}\,P_{\rm init}+\sqrt{1-e^{-h}}\,\zeta_{0}]  and  [X_{t_{j}}^{[0]}\coloneqq X_{\rm init}+t_{j}P_{0}] .

2. 

@S3.I1.i2.p1 

@S3.I1.i2.p1.1 Perform the first Picard update:





 [\displaystyle X_{t_{i}}^{[1]}] 
 [\displaystyle\coloneqq X_{\rm init}+t_{i}P_{0}-\sum_{j=1}^{J}\omega_{i,j}\,\widehat{g}_{\eta}(X_{t_{j}}^{[0]};G_{j}^{[0]})\,.] 



3. 

@S3.I1.i3.p1 

@S3.I1.i3.p1.1 Perform the second Picard update:





 [\displaystyle X_{h}^{[2]}] 
 [\displaystyle\coloneqq X_{\rm init}+hP_{0}-\sum_{j=1}^{J}\omega_{J,j}\,\widehat{g}_{\eta}(X_{t_{j}}^{[1]};G_{j}^{[1]})\,,\qquad P_{h}^{[2]}\coloneqq P_{0}-\sum_{j=1}^{J}\omega_{j}\,\widehat{g}_{\eta}(X_{t_{j}}^{[1]};G_{j}^{[1]})\,.] 



4. 

@S3.I1.i4.p1 

@S3.I1.i4.p1.1 Partially refresh the momentum and initialize the next phase.


@S3.I1.i4.p2 

@S3.I1.i4.p2.1 Set  [X_{\rm init}\coloneqq X_{h}^{[2]}]  and  [P_{\rm init}\coloneqq e^{-h/2}\,P_{h}^{[2]}+\sqrt{1-e^{-h}}\,\zeta_{1}] .



@S3.Thmtheorem1.p1.2 After the prescribed number of phases, return  [Y\coloneqq X_{\rm init}] .



@S3.SS2.SSS0.Px2.p4 

@S3.SS2.SSS0.Px2.p4.1 Finally, recall from Section 3.1 that once we have an approximate sample from the smoothed distribution  [\pi_{\eta}] , we can recover an approximate sample from  [\pi]  by sampling from the RGO.
We implement this last step via FORS, leading to the full algorithm.


@S3.Thmtheorem2 
Algorithm 3.2 (Smoothed Picard HMC with a terminal FORS step).

@S3.Thmtheorem2.p1 

@S3.Thmtheorem2.p1.1 Given the final position  [Y]  of Algorithm 3.1, independently run the terminal
FORS routine of Theorem A.4 with target  [R_{\eta,Y}] .
Return
its output  [X_{\rm out}] .



@S3.SS2.SSS0.Px2.p5 

@S3.SS2.SSS0.Px2.p5.1 The exact guarantee is Theorem 1.1, and it is proven in Section 4.
We next describe some of the ideas of the analysis at a high level in order to explain the source of the  [d^{1/6}]  scaling.
For the sake of exposition, we ignore the dependence on  [\kappa]  and  [\varepsilon] .



@S3.SS2.SSS0.Px3 
Analysis.


@S3.SS2.SSS0.Px3.p1 

@S3.SS2.SSS0.Px3.p1.1 The analysis is based on the local error framework [46], which produces multi-step  [W_{2}]  bounds via computations of “local” or one-step errors.
The key is to decompose the one-step error into “deterministic” and “stochastic” components.
Over the course of  [\asymp h^{-1}]  iterations, the former is expected to accrue with a prefactor  [h^{-1}] , whereas the latter only accrues with a prefactor  [h^{-1/2}]  due to cancellations.
In our analysis, the “deterministic” component consists of two terms: the bias of our stochastic gradient estimator, since  [\mathbb{E}\widehat{g}_{\eta}(\cdot;G)\neq g_{\eta}] , and the discretization error of the Picard integrator.
The “stochastic” component consists of the fluctuations, or variance, produced by the stochastic gradient estimator.
We handle these terms individually.

• 

@S3.I2.i1.p1 

@S3.I2.i1.p1.1 Discretization error.
Here, the error further consists of two parts: the quadrature error, and the error of the Picard iteration itself.
They are controlled in Proposition 4.15.

– 

@S3.I2.i1.I1.i1.p1 

@S3.I2.i1.I1.i1.p1.1 Quadrature error. It is classical that the error of approximating an integral via numerical quadrature depends strongly on the smoothness of the integrand.
In our setting, this amounts to the smoothness of  [V_{\eta}] .
By studying Gaussian cumulants, we present a refined estimate showing that the size of  [\lVert D^{k}V_{\eta}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}]  is of order at most  [\sqrt{d}\,\eta^{-(k-2)/2}] , for  [k\geqslant 2]  (Lemma 4.3).
From this bound, we are able to show that the quadrature error is bounded by a term involving  [(h/\sqrt{\eta})^{J}] , where  [J]  is the number of Chebyshev–Lobatto nodes.
By taking polylogarithmic  [J] , this error is made negligible, provided  [h<\sqrt{\eta}] .
This shows that the natural scale is to take  [h\asymp\sqrt{\eta}] , and we henceforth impose this choice in order to facilitate the interpretation of the subsequent bounds.

– 

@S3.I2.i1.I1.i2.p1 

@S3.I2.i1.I1.i2.p1.1 Picard error.
The error of Picard iteration decreases exponentially with the depth.
We show that already for the depth- [2]  scheme, this error is of order  [\sqrt{d}\,h^{5}] , which is negligible compared to the other error terms.



• 

@S3.I2.i2.p1 

@S3.I2.i2.p1.1 Bias. Deterministically, we have  [\lVert\mathbb{E}\widehat{g}_{\eta}(\cdot;G)-g_{\eta}\rVert\leqslant\eta^{3/2}\sqrt{d}]  (Lemma 4.2).
Propagating this through the dynamics, it is straightforward to show that the bias error is of order  [\sqrt{d}\,(h\eta^{3/2}+\eta^{1/2}h^{3})\asymp\sqrt{d}\,h^{4}]  (Proposition 4.9).

• 

@S3.I2.i3.p1 

@S3.I2.i3.p1.1 Variance. As shown in [52], sharp analyses of stochastic gradient samplers should leverage the fact that centered perturbations of a standard Gaussian are closer in  [W_{2}]  to the Gaussian than a naïve coupling argument would predict; in fact, in this case, the  [W_{2}]  distance scales with the square of the size of the perturbation.
We refer to this type of result as a second-order  [W_{2}]  estimate.
We extend the convolution bound of [52] to a more general setting in Lemma 4.11, based on a novel heat flow interpolation argument.
We use this to prove a bound on the variance error term of order  [\sqrt{d}\,(\eta h^{3/2}+\eta^{1/2}h^{3})\lesssim\sqrt{d}\,h^{7/2}]  (Proposition 4.13).




@S3.SS2.SSS0.Px3.p2 

@S3.SS2.SSS0.Px3.p2.1 After accumulating these errors through the local error framework, we find that the multi-step error is of order  [\sqrt{d}\,h^{3}] , leading to the scaling  [h\asymp d^{-1/6}] .


@S3.SS2.SSS0.Px3.p3 

@S3.SS2.SSS0.Px3.p3.1 Finally, since smoothed Picard HMC step targets the smoothed distribution  [\pi_{\eta}] , we need a terminal step in which we sample from the RGO  [R_{\eta}] .
As shown in Appendix A, when implemented with the exact diffusion sampler of [17], the cost of this step is  [\widetilde{O}(1+(\eta^{2}d)^{1/3})] .
At our scale  [\eta\asymp d^{-1/3}] , this terminal step only costs  [\widetilde{O}(d^{1/9})]  queries, so it is not dominant.




@S3.SS3 

3.3 Higher moment control


@S3.SS3.p1 

@S3.SS3.p1.1 For our warm start construction, we need a stronger  [W_{q}]  guarantee,  [q\geqslant 2] , for smoothed Picard HMC.
We establish the following result.


@Thminformalthmx1 
Informal theorem (see Theorem 5.1 for precise statement).

@Thminformalthmx1.p1 

@Thminformalthmx1.p1.1 Suppose that  [\kappa^{-1}I\preceq\nabla^{2}V\preceq I]  and we are given a reference point
 [x_{\rm ref}]  satisfying  [\lVert\nabla V(x_{\rm ref})\rVert\leqslant\sqrt{d/\kappa}] .
For any  [q\geqslant 2] ,  [0<\varepsilon\leqslant 1] , and fixed  [c_{0}>0] , gradient-only smoothed
Picard HMC can choose a smoothing variance  [0<\eta\leqslant c_{0}]  and return a law
 [\widehat{\pi}_{\eta}]  satisfying  [W_{q}(\widehat{\pi}_{\eta},\pi_{\eta})\leqslant\varepsilon] , using





 [O\Bigl(\Bigl\{\kappa^{2}+\frac{\kappa^{4/3}\,(d+q)^{1/6}}{\varepsilon^{1/3}}\Bigr\}\,\Bigl\{q+\log\frac{e\kappa d}{\varepsilon}\Bigr\}^{6}\Bigr)] 



@Thminformalthmx1.p1.2 gradient queries in expectation, with an implicit constant depending only on
 [c_{0}] .



@S3.SS3.p2 

@S3.SS3.p2.1 This is taken up in Section 5 and follows the same outline as the analysis in Section 4, except that we need several  [L^{q}]  extensions of the ingredients from Section 4 which can be somewhat subtle.
Note that this theorem only guarantees closeness to the smoothed distribution  [\pi_{\eta}] , but this is all that is required for our warm start generator described in Section 3.4.


@S3.SS3.p3 

@S3.SS3.p3.1 We highlight in particular that Lemma 5.3 establishes an  [L^{q}]  extension of the classical local error framework, which could be useful for future works.


@S3.SS3.p4 

@S3.SS3.p4.1 Note that when we upgrade the  [W_{2}]  guarantee in Theorem 1.1 to a  [W_{q}]  guarantee, we pay a price which is polynomial in  [q] .
However, for our warm start application, we only need to take  [q]  to be logarithmic in the problem parameters, so this turns out to be harmless.



@S3.SS4 

3.4 Recursive warm start generation


@S3.SS4.p1 

@S3.SS4.p1.1 Next, we develop a powerful recursive framework for warm start generation.
Here, we suppose we have access to a sampling subroutine which we call a smoothed sampler.
Given a strongly convex and smooth potential  [V] , normalized to satisfy  [\kappa^{-1}I\preceq\nabla^{2}V\preceq I] , as well as an admissible reference point  [x_{\rm ref}]  with  [\lVert\nabla V(x_{\rm ref})\rVert\leqslant\sqrt{d/\kappa}] , the smoothed sampler returns a smoothing level  [0\leqslant\eta\ll 1]  and a sample which is  [\varepsilon] -close in law to  [\pi_{V}*\mathsf{N}(0,\eta I)] , where  [\pi_{V}\propto\exp(-V)] .


@S3.SS4.p2 

@S3.SS4.p2.1 We remark that if  [V]  satisfies  [\alpha I\preceq\nabla^{2}V\preceq\beta I] , then we can apply the smoothed sampler to  [V(\cdot/\sqrt{\beta})] , and then rescale the output by  [\beta^{-1/2}] .
After this rescaling, the reported smoothing level  [\eta]  corresponds to the law of the rescaled sample being close to  [\pi_{V}*\mathsf{N}(0,\beta^{-1}\eta I)] .


@S3.SS4.p3 

@S3.SS4.p3.1 We consider three types of guarantees for the smoothed sampler: the output law is close to the smoothed distribution in  [W_{2}] , in  [W_{p}]  (for  [p\geqslant 2] ), or in the sub-Gaussian Orlicz–Wasserstein metric  [W_{\psi_{2}}] .
In these three cases, by taking advantage of regularization along the heat flow, we develop a recursive sampler that upgrades the guarantee to  [\KL] , to mixed  [\mathsf{TV}]  and  [\mathsf{R}_{q}]  (as in the guarantee of Theorem 1.2), and to  [\mathsf{R}_{q}] , respectively.


@S3.SS4.p4 

@S3.SS4.p4.1 For  [V\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})] ,  [A>0] , and  [u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}] , consider the RGO potential and distribution





 [\displaystyle V_{A,u}] 
 [\displaystyle\coloneqq V+\frac{\lVert\cdot-u\rVert^{2}}{2A}\,,\qquad R^{V}_{A,u}\propto\exp(-V_{A,u})\,.] 



@S3.SS4.p4.2 The following recursive sampler aims to draw an approximate sample from the RGO distribution  [R^{V}_{A,u}] .
Note that when  [A=\infty] , this just corresponds to  [\pi\propto\exp(-V)] . The sampling task is easier for smaller  [A]  as  [V_{A,u}]  then has a smaller condition number. The trick of the following algorithm is to recursively reduce the RGO sampling task to one with a smaller  [A] , until the terminal level is reached.


@S3.Thmtheorem3 
Algorithm 3.3 (Recursive RGO sampler).

@S3.Thmtheorem3.p1 

@S3.Thmtheorem3.p1.1 Fix a terminal level  [\underline{A}>0] .

• 

@S3.I3.i1.p1 

@S3.I3.i1.p1.1 If  [A\leqslant\underline{A}] , then simply apply the localized FORS routine in Theorem A.4 directly to  [R_{A,u}^{V}] .

• 

@S3.I3.i2.p1 

@S3.I3.i2.p1.1 Otherwise, proceed via the following steps.

– 

@S3.I3.i2.I1.i1.p1 

@S3.I3.i2.I1.i1.p1.1 Apply the smoothed sampler to  [V_{A,u}] , and let  [\eta>0]  be the reported smoothing level, and let  [Z]  be the rescaled output.
Thus,  [\law(Z)\approx R_{A,u}^{V}*\mathsf{N}(0,\beta_{A}^{-1}\eta I)] , where  [\beta_{A}]  is the smoothness of  [V_{A,u}] .

– 

@S3.I3.i2.I1.i2.p1 

@S3.I3.i2.I1.i2.p1.1 Pick  [\tau>0] , draw an independent
 [G\sim\mathsf{N}(0,I)] , and form





 [Y\coloneqq Z+\sqrt{\frac{\tau}{\beta_{A}}}\,G\,,\qquad a\coloneqq\frac{\eta+\tau}{\beta_{A}}\,.] 



@S3.I3.i2.I1.i2.p1.2 Thus,  [\law(Y)\approx R^{V}_{A,u}*\mathsf{N}(0,aI)] .

– 

@S3.I3.i2.I1.i3.p1 

@S3.I3.i2.I1.i3.p1.1 We wish to obtain an approximate sample from  [R^{V}_{A,u}] . It suffices to sample from  [R^{V_{A,u}}_{a,Y}] .
Indeed, by the definition of the RGO, for any potential  [V] , we have the identity  [X\sim\pi_{V}]  if  [Y\sim\pi_{V}*\mathsf{N}(0,aI)]  and  [X\sim R^{V}_{a,Y}] .


@S3.I3.i2.I1.i3.p2 

@S3.I3.i2.I1.i3.p2.1 In Lemma 6.4, we justify the following closure property of the RGO:  [R_{a,Y}^{V_{A,u}}=R^{V}_{A^{+},u^{+}}] , where the updated parameters are





 [\frac{1}{A^{+}}\coloneqq\frac{1}{A}+\frac{1}{a}\,,\qquad u^{+}\coloneqq A^{+}\,\Bigl(\frac{u}{A}+\frac{Y}{a}\Bigr)\,.] 



@S3.I3.i2.I1.i3.p2.2 Thus, we call the recursive RGO sampler on  [R^{V}_{A^{+},u^{+}}] , and
return its output.







@S3.SS4.p5 

@S3.SS4.p5.1 At this point, it may be unclear why we add an additional Gaussian to the output of the smoothed sampler.
In fact, this is the key step which enables the use of regularization along the heat flow—specifically, in the form of reverse transport inequalities—to upgrade the smoothed sampler guarantee from Wasserstein to stronger distances.
The guarantee for Algorithm 3.3 is given in Theorem 6.5.


@S3.SS4.p6 

@S3.SS4.p6.1 We can apply Algorithm 3.3 to sample from  [\pi]  by taking  [A=\infty] .
However, if the iteration complexity of the smoothed sampler has poor dependence on the condition number, then it is better to wrap the entire recursive scheme within the proximal sampler [37, 19].


@S3.Thmtheorem4 
Algorithm 3.4 (Proximal sampler wrapper).

@S3.Thmtheorem4.p1 

@S3.Thmtheorem4.p1.1 Given an initial sample  [X_{0}] , an outer variance  [a_{\rm out}>0] ,
and a number  [N]  of outer steps, iterate as follows. At step
 [n\in\{0,\ldots,N-1\}] , draw
 [Y_{n}=X_{n}+\mathsf{N}(0,a_{\rm out}I)] , and set  [X_{n+1}]  to be the
output of Algorithm 3.3 with target
 [R^{V}_{a_{\rm out},Y_{n}}] . Return the final iterate  [X_{N}] .



@S3.SS4.p7 

@S3.SS4.p7.1 This wrapper ensures that we only ever call the smoothed sampler on well-conditioned targets, leading to an overall improved condition number dependence.
This aligns with the original use of the proximal sampler in [37].
The full guarantee for the recursive warm start generator is given in
Theorem 6.1.
We emphasize that the guarantee is fully generic and applies to any smoothed sampler implementing our interface.


@S3.SS4.p8 

@S3.SS4.p8.1 Instantiating the recursive warm start generator with smoothed Picard HMC as a subroutine then establishes our warm start guarantee in Theorem 1.2.




@S4 

4 Proof of the  [W_{2}]  guarantee


@S4.p1 

@S4.p1.1 In this section, our goal is to prove the  [W_{2}]  guarantee (Theorem 1.1). However, since we will later need to obtain stronger moment estimates for Theorem 1.2, we will first develop the part of the analysis that is common to both results.


@S4.SS1 

4.1 Setup and smoothing estimates


@S4.SS1.p1 

@S4.SS1.p1.1 For a positive-definite matrix  [M] , write





 [W_{q,M}(P,Q)\coloneqq\inf_{X\sim P,\,Y\sim Q}\bigl(\mathbb{E}\lVert X-Y\rVert_{M}^{q}\bigr)^{1/q}\,,\qquad\lVert x\rVert_{M}\coloneqq(x^{\mathsf{T}}Mx)^{1/2}\,.] 



@S4.SS1.p1.2 Recall that





 [\pi_{\eta}\coloneqq\pi*\mathsf{N}(0,\eta I)\,,\qquad\Pi_{\eta}(\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}p)\coloneqq\pi_{\eta}(\mathrm{d}x)\,\mathsf{N}(0,I)(\mathrm{d}p)\,.] 



@S4.SS1.p1.3 Let  [X\mid Y=y]  be drawn from  [R_{\eta,y}] .
Recalling the definition (2.1) of the proximal operator, throughout this section we write





 [x_{y}^{+}\coloneqq\operatorname{prox}_{\eta V}(y)\,.] 



@S4.SS1.p1.4 It is easy to check the identities





 [g_{\eta}(y)=\nabla V_{\eta}(y)=\eta^{-1}\,(y-\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=y])\,,\qquad\nabla^{2}V_{\eta}(y)=\eta^{-1}I-\eta^{-2}\operatorname{Cov}(X\mid Y=y)\,.] 

(4.1)



@S4.Thmtheorem1 
Lemma 4.1 (Hessian bounds for the smoothed potential).

@S4.Thmtheorem1.p1 

@S4.Thmtheorem1.p1.1 For  [0<\eta\leqslant 1] ,





 [\frac{1}{\kappa+\eta}\,I\preceq\nabla^{2}V_{\eta}\preceq\frac{1}{1+\eta}\,I\preceq I\,.] 



@S4.Thmtheorem1.p1.2 In particular  [g_{\eta}]  is  [1] -Lipschitz.



@S4.SS1.2 
Proof.

@S4.SS1.p2 

@S4.SS1.p2.1 The RGO potential has Hessian between
 [(\kappa^{-1}+\eta^{-1})\,I]  and  [(1+\eta^{-1})\,I] . The
Brascamp–Lieb covariance inequality [13] gives





 [\operatorname{Cov}(X\mid Y=y)\preceq(\kappa^{-1}+\eta^{-1})^{-1}I\,.] 



@S4.SS1.p2.2 For the reverse bound, the
Cramér–Rao inequality (see, e.g., [24, Theorem 3.5.8]) gives





 [\operatorname{Cov}(X\mid Y=y)\succeq(1+\eta^{-1})^{-1}I\,.] 



@S4.SS1.p2.3 Substitute both bounds in
(4.1).
∎



@S4.SS1.p3 

@S4.SS1.p3.1 The following is the key estimate that decomposes the stochastic gradient error into a bias term and a stochastic error term.


@S4.Thmtheorem2 
Lemma 4.2 (Stochastic gradient estimates).

@S4.Thmtheorem2.p1 

@S4.Thmtheorem2.p1.1 Write





 [\widehat{g}_{\eta}(y;G)\coloneqq g_{\eta}(y)+b_{\eta}(y)+\xi_{\eta}(y;G)\,,\qquad\mathbb{E}\xi_{\eta}(y;G)=0\,.] 



@S4.Thmtheorem2.p1.2 Then, for all  [u,y,y^{\prime},G,G^{\prime}] ,





 [\displaystyle\lVert b_{\eta}(y)\rVert] 
 [\displaystyle\leqslant\eta^{3/2}\sqrt{d}\,,] 

(4.2)



 [\displaystyle\mathbb{E}e^{\langle u,\xi_{\eta}(y;G)\rangle}] 
 [\displaystyle\leqslant e^{\eta\,\lVert u\rVert^{2}/2}\,,] 

(4.3)



 [\displaystyle\lVert\widehat{g}_{\eta}(y;G)-\widehat{g}_{\eta}(y^{\prime};G)\rVert] 
 [\displaystyle\leqslant\lVert y-y^{\prime}\rVert\,,] 

(4.4)



 [\displaystyle\lVert\widehat{g}_{\eta}(y;G)-\widehat{g}_{\eta}(y;G^{\prime})\rVert] 
 [\displaystyle\leqslant\sqrt{\eta}\,\lVert G-G^{\prime}\rVert\,.] 

(4.5)




@S4.SS1.3 
Proof.

@S4.SS1.p4 

@S4.SS1.p4.1 Proximal optimality gives
 [\nabla V(x_{y}^{+})=(y-x_{y}^{+})/\eta] . Define





 [\rho_{y}(u)\coloneqq V(x_{y}^{+}+\sqrt{\eta}u)-V(x_{y}^{+})-\sqrt{\eta}\,\langle\nabla V(x_{y}^{+}),u\rangle\,.] 



@S4.SS1.p4.2 Then  [0\preceq\nabla^{2}\rho_{y}\preceq\eta I] , and under  [R_{\eta,y}] ,
 [(X-x_{y}^{+})/\sqrt{\eta}]  has density
proportional to  [u\mapsto e^{-\lVert u\rVert^{2}/2-\rho_{y}(u)}] . Call this law  [\mathsf{r}_{y}] .
We have





 [W_{2}(\mathsf{r}_{y},\mathsf{N}(0,I))\leqslant\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{r}_{y}}[\lVert\nabla\rho_{y}(U)\rVert^{2}]}\leqslant\eta\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{r}_{y}}\lVert U\rVert^{2}}\leqslant\eta\sqrt{d}\,.] 

(4.6)


@S4.SS1.p4.3 Indeed, the first inequality combines Talagrand’s inequality for the standard
Gaussian with the Gaussian log-Sobolev inequality; the second uses that
 [\nabla\rho_{y}(0)=0]  and  [\nabla\rho_{y}]  is  [\eta] -Lipschitz; and the last
follows by integration by parts since  [\mathsf{r}_{y}]  is  [1] -strongly
log-concave with its mode at  [0] .


@S4.SS1.p5 

@S4.SS1.p5.1 Moreover,





 [g_{\eta}(y)=\nabla V(x_{y}^{+})+\eta^{-1/2}\,\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{r}_{y}}\nabla\rho_{y}(U)\,,\qquad\widehat{g}_{\eta}(y;G)=\nabla V(x_{y}^{+})+\eta^{-1/2}\,\nabla\rho_{y}(G)\,.] 



@S4.SS1.p5.2 The gradient of  [\rho_{y}]  is  [\eta] -Lipschitz, so
(4.6) gives (4.2). Since  [\widehat{g}_{\eta}(y;\cdot)]  is  [\sqrt{\eta}] -Lipschitz, the standard Gaussian concentration inequality
gives (4.3), and the same observation gives
(4.5). Finally,  [y\mapsto x_{y}^{+}]  is non-expansive and
 [\nabla V]  is  [1] -Lipschitz, proving (4.4).
∎



@S4.SS1.p6 

@S4.SS1.p6.1 We will also need high-order regularity of the smoothed potential. The key
point is a near-Gaussian cancellation, which is stronger than the generic moment bound for a
strongly log-concave law. The Gaussian cumulant estimates used in the proof
are collected in Appendix C.


@S4.Thmtheorem3 
Lemma 4.3 (Derivatives of the smoothed potential).

@S4.Thmtheorem3.p1 

@S4.Thmtheorem3.p1.1 For every integer  [k\geqslant 2] ,





 [\displaystyle\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\,\lVert D^{k}V_{\eta}(y)\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}] 
 [\displaystyle\leqslant(k-1)!\,\sqrt{d}\,\eta^{-(k-2)/2}\,,] 

(4.7)



 [\displaystyle\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\,\lVert v\rVert=1}\,\lVert D^{k}V_{\eta}(y)[v,\cdot^{k-1}]\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}] 
 [\displaystyle\leqslant(k-1)!\,\eta^{-(k-2)/2}\,.] 

(4.8)


@S4.Thmtheorem3.p1.2 Consequently,





 [\lVert\nabla^{2}V_{\eta}(y)-\nabla^{2}V_{\eta}(y^{\prime})\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}\leqslant 2\eta^{-1/2}\,\lVert y-y^{\prime}\rVert\,.] 

(4.9)




@S4.SS1.4 
Proof.

@S4.SS1.p7 

@S4.SS1.p7.1 Let  [\mathsf{r}_{y}]  be the normalized RGO from the proof of
Lemma 4.2. Its potential has Hessian between
 [I]  and  [(1+\eta)I] . Let  [T_{y}]  be the optimal transport
from the standard Gaussian to  [\mathsf{r}_{y}] . Caffarelli’s contraction theorem
[14], applied in both directions, gives





 [(1+\eta)^{-1/2}I\preceq DT_{y}\preceq I\,,\qquad\lVert DT_{y}-I\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant\eta/2\,,] 

(4.10)


@S4.SS1.p7.2 almost everywhere.


@S4.SS1.p8 

@S4.SS1.p8.1 By (C.22), for  [k\geqslant 3] ,





 [D^{k}V_{\eta}(y)=-\eta^{-k}\operatorname{Cum}_{k}(X\mid Y=y)\,.] 



@S4.SS1.p8.2 Under the RGO,
 [X=x_{y}^{+}+\sqrt{\eta}\,T_{y}(G)] ,  [G\sim\mathsf{N}(0,I)] . Translation invariance and homogeneity of
cumulants, followed by (4.10) and
Lemma C.6 with  [\varepsilon_{T}\leqslant\eta/2] , prove
(4.7)–(4.8).
The case  [k=2]  is Lemma 4.1; integrating the
 [k=3]  estimate (4.8) along a segment gives
(4.9).
∎




@S4.SS2 

4.2 Local error analysis


@S4.SS2.p1 

@S4.SS2.p1.1 The  [W_{2}]  proof will be based on the local error framework.


@S4.Thmtheorem4 
Lemma 4.4 (Local error framework).

@S4.Thmtheorem4.p1 

@S4.Thmtheorem4.p1.1 Let  [P,\widehat{P}]  be Markov kernels on  [\mathbb{R}^{m}] . Suppose that, for every
 [x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{m}] , there is a coupling
 [X\sim\delta_{x}P] ,  [Y\sim\delta_{y}P]  such that





 [\lVert X-Y\rVert_{L^{2}}\leqslant\rho\,\lVert x-y\rVert\,,\qquad\lVert(X-x)-(Y-y)\rVert_{L^{2}}\leqslant\chi\,\lVert x-y\rVert\,.] 

(4.11)


@S4.Thmtheorem4.p1.2 Suppose also that, writing  [\widehat{X}\sim\delta_{x}\widehat{P}] ,





 [W_{2}(\delta_{x}\widehat{P},\delta_{x}P)\leqslant\mathcal{E}_{\rm strong}\,,\qquad\lVert\mathbb{E}\widehat{X}-\mathbb{E}X\rVert\leqslant\mathcal{E}_{\rm weak}\,.] 



@S4.Thmtheorem4.p1.3 Assume  [0<\rho<1] . Then, for all probability laws  [\mu,\nu]  on
 [\mathbb{R}^{m}] ,





 [W_{2}^{2}(\mu\widehat{P},\nu P)\leqslant\rho W_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)+\mathcal{E}_{\rm strong}^{2}+\frac{(\mathcal{E}_{\rm weak}+\chi\mathcal{E}_{\rm strong})^{2}}{\rho\,(1-\rho)}\,.] 





@S4.SS2.2 
Proof.

@S4.SS2.p2 

@S4.SS2.p2.1 This is the standard local error theorem
[24, Lemma 5.1.2].
∎



@S4.SS2.p3 

@S4.SS2.p3.1 We introduce three kernels and associated mappings.

• 

@S4.I1.i1.p1 

@S4.I1.i1.p1.1 Let  [\widehat{K}]  denote the one-phase
kernel defined by Algorithm 3.1 with
stochastic gradients  [\widehat{g}_{\eta}] . We can express the output of the phase as
 [\widehat{\Phi}_{z}(\zeta,G)]  for Gaussian variables  [\zeta] ,  [G] .

• 

@S4.I1.i2.p1 

@S4.I1.i2.p1.1 Define





 [\bar{\Phi}_{z}(\zeta)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[\widehat{\Phi}_{z}(\zeta,G)\mid\zeta]\,,\qquad\bar{K}(z,\cdot)\coloneqq(\bar{\Phi}_{z})_{\#}\mathsf{N}(0,I_{2d})\,.] 



• 

@S4.I1.i3.p1 

@S4.I1.i3.p1.1 Finally, let  [K]  be the exact gradient numerical kernel obtained by
replacing every stochastic gradient  [\widehat{g}_{\eta}]  by  [g_{\eta}] . Write  [\Phi_{z}]  for the corresponding map, so that





 [K(z,\cdot)\coloneqq(\Phi_{z})_{\#}\mathsf{N}(0,I_{2d})\,.] 






@S4.SS2.p4 

@S4.SS2.p4.1 It is standard that contraction for HMC is only witnessed after a change of coordinates. Set





 [M_{\kappa}\coloneqq\begin{bmatrix}\frac{1}{2\kappa}+\frac{1}{2}&\frac{1}{2}\\[2.84526pt]
\frac{1}{2}&1\end{bmatrix}\otimes I_{d}\,.] 



@S4.SS2.p4.2 Since  [\kappa\geqslant 1] , this defines a metric which is uniformly equivalent to the Euclidean norm; such twisted metrics are standard in the analysis of kinetic dynamics; see, e.g., [23, 30].
We apply the lemma after the linear change of coordinates
 [z\mapsto M_{\kappa}^{1/2}z] , with  [P\coloneqq K]  and
 [\widehat{P}\coloneqq\widehat{K}] .
The structure of the proof is given by the following key theorem.


@S4.Thmtheorem5 
Theorem 4.5 (Local error analysis in  [W_{2}] ).

@S4.Thmtheorem5.p1 

@S4.Thmtheorem5.p1.1 Let  [J\geqslant 2] , and assume that  [h\leqslant c/(\kappa\log J)] .
Then, for every phase-space law  [\widehat{\mu}] ,





 [W_{2,M_{\kappa}}^{2}(\widehat{\mu}\widehat{K},\Pi_{\eta})\leqslant(1-ch/\kappa)\,W_{2,M_{\kappa}}^{2}(\widehat{\mu},\Pi_{\eta})+C\mathcal{E}_{{\rm var}}^{2}+\frac{C\kappa}{h}\,(\mathcal{E}_{{\rm bias}}^{2}+\mathcal{E}_{{\rm disc}}^{2})\,,] 



@S4.Thmtheorem5.p1.2 where we define





 [\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{{\rm bias}}] 
 [\displaystyle\coloneqq\sup_{z,\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\,\lVert\bar{\Phi}_{z}(\zeta)-\Phi_{z}(\zeta)\rVert\,,\quad\mathcal{E}_{{\rm var}}\coloneqq\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}}W_{2}(\delta_{z}\widehat{K},\delta_{z}\bar{K})\,,\quad\mathcal{E}_{{\rm disc}}\coloneqq W_{2}(\Pi_{\eta}K,\Pi_{\eta})\,.] 



@S4.Thmtheorem5.p1.3 Consequently,





 [\displaystyle W_{2,M_{\kappa}}^{2}(\widehat{\mu}\widehat{K}^{N},\Pi_{\eta})] 
 [\displaystyle\leqslant e^{-cNh/\kappa}\,W_{2,M_{\kappa}}^{2}(\widehat{\mu},\Pi_{\eta})+\frac{C\kappa}{h}\,\mathcal{E}_{{\rm var}}^{2}+\frac{C\kappa^{2}}{h^{2}}\,(\mathcal{E}_{{\rm bias}}^{2}+\mathcal{E}_{{\rm disc}}^{2})\,.] 

(4.12)




@S4.SS2.3 
Proof.

@S4.SS2.p5 

@S4.SS2.p5.1 Fix  [z,z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}]  and set
 [Z\coloneqq\Phi_{z}(\zeta)] ,  [Z^{\prime}\coloneqq\Phi_{z^{\prime}}(\zeta)] .
Since  [J\geqslant 2]  and
 [\Lambda_{J}\leqslant C\log(J+1)\leqslant C\log J] , the assumption on  [h]  implies
the hypotheses of
Propositions 4.7 and 4.8 below.
Those results verify (4.11) with
 [(Z,Z^{\prime})]  in place of  [(X,Y)] . We can bound





 [\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\rm weak}] 
 [\displaystyle\coloneqq\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\,\lVert\mathbb{E}\widehat{\Phi}_{z}(\zeta,G)-\mathbb{E}\Phi_{z}(\zeta)\rVert_{M_{\kappa}}\leqslant\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\mathbb{E}\lVert\bar{\Phi}_{z}(\zeta)-\Phi_{z}(\zeta)\rVert_{M_{\kappa}}\leqslant C\mathcal{E}_{{\rm bias}}\,,] 



@S4.SS2.p5.2 and





 [\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\rm strong}] 
 [\displaystyle\coloneqq\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}}W_{2,M_{\kappa}}(\delta_{z}\widehat{K},\delta_{z}K)\leqslant\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\,\{W_{2,M_{\kappa}}(\delta_{z}\widehat{K},\delta_{z}\bar{K})+W_{2,M_{\kappa}}(\delta_{z}\bar{K},\delta_{z}K)\}] 





 [\displaystyle\leqslant C\,(\mathcal{E}_{{\rm var}}+\mathcal{E}_{{\rm bias}})\,,] 



@S4.SS2.p5.3 since the  [M_{\kappa}]  norm is equivalent to the standard Euclidean norm.
Thus, the hypotheses of
Lemma 4.4 hold with





 [\rho\coloneqq 1-\frac{ch}{\kappa}\,,\qquad\chi\coloneqq Ch\,,\qquad\mathcal{E}_{\rm strong}\leqslant C\,(\mathcal{E}_{{\rm var}}+\mathcal{E}_{{\rm bias}})\,,\qquad\mathcal{E}_{\rm weak}\leqslant C\mathcal{E}_{{\rm bias}}\,.] 



@S4.SS2.p5.4 We obtain





 [W_{2,M_{\kappa}}^{2}(\widehat{\mu}\widehat{K},\Pi_{\eta}K)\leqslant\Bigl(1-\frac{ch}{\kappa}\Bigr)\,W_{2,M_{\kappa}}^{2}(\widehat{\mu},\Pi_{\eta})+C\mathcal{E}_{{\rm var}}^{2}+\frac{C\kappa}{h}\,\mathcal{E}_{{\rm bias}}^{2}\,.] 



@S4.SS2.p5.5 By Young’s inequality, for  [\lambda>0] ,





 [\displaystyle W_{2,M_{\kappa}}^{2}(\widehat{\mu}\widehat{K},\Pi_{\eta})] 
 [\displaystyle\leqslant(1+\lambda)\,W_{2,M_{\kappa}}^{2}(\widehat{\mu}\widehat{K},\Pi_{\eta}K)+(1+\lambda^{-1})\,W_{2,M_{\kappa}}^{2}(\Pi_{\eta}K,\Pi_{\eta})\,.] 



@S4.SS2.p5.6 Uniform norm equivalence gives
 [W_{2,M_{\kappa}}(\Pi_{\eta}K,\Pi_{\eta})\leqslant C\mathcal{E}_{{\rm disc}}] .
Choose  [\lambda\asymp h/\kappa] . After reducing
the universal constant in the contraction factor  [ch/\kappa] , this proves the one-step
bound; summing the recurrence proves the global bound.
∎



@S4.SS2.p6 

@S4.SS2.p6.1 The three errors  [\mathcal{E}_{{\rm bias}}] ,  [\mathcal{E}_{{\rm var}}] , and  [\mathcal{E}_{{\rm disc}}]  correspond,
respectively, to the bias and centered fluctuation of the stochastic gradient
phase and to the discretization error of the numerical kernel. Thus, the
main task is to bound these errors.



@S4.SS3 

4.3 Properties of the exact-gradient kernel


@S4.SS3.p1 

@S4.SS3.p1.1 Write  [a\coloneqq e^{-h/2}] , and for a symmetric matrix  [H] ,
let





 [A_{H}\coloneqq\begin{bmatrix}0&I\\
-H&-I\end{bmatrix}\,.] 



@S4.SS3.p1.2 In the lemma below, we make use of the Lebesgue constant  [\Lambda_{J}]  for the Chebyshev–Lobatto nodes.
By Proposition B.1, we have  [\Lambda_{J}\lesssim\log J] .


@S4.Thmtheorem6 
Lemma 4.6 (First-order difference expansion).

@S4.Thmtheorem6.p1 

@S4.Thmtheorem6.p1.1 Couple draws  [Z_{1}\sim\delta_{z}K]  and  [Z_{1}^{\prime}\sim\delta_{z^{\prime}}K]  from
phase-space inputs
 [z\coloneqq(x_{\rm init},p_{\rm init})]  and
 [z^{\prime}\coloneqq(x^{\prime}_{\rm init},p^{\prime}_{\rm init})]  using the same Gaussian variables.
Set





 [\Delta_{0}\coloneqq z-z^{\prime}\,,\qquad\Delta_{1}\coloneqq Z_{1}-Z_{1}^{\prime}\,.] 



@S4.Thmtheorem6.p1.2 If  [h^{2}\Lambda_{J}\leqslant 1] , then





 [\Delta_{1}=(I+hA_{H})\Delta_{0}+\mathcal{R}_{h}\Delta_{0}\,,\qquad\lVert\mathcal{R}_{h}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant C\Lambda_{J}h^{2}\,,] 



@S4.Thmtheorem6.p1.3 for a symmetric  [H]  with
 [\frac{1}{2\kappa}\,I\preceq H\preceq I] .



@S4.SS3.2 
Proof.

@S4.SS3.p2 

@S4.SS3.p2.1 To verify this, write
 [\Delta x\coloneqq x_{\rm init}-x^{\prime}_{\rm init}]  and
 [\Delta p\coloneqq p_{\rm init}-p^{\prime}_{\rm init}] .
The difference of gradients can be written as





 [g_{\eta}(x)-g_{\eta}(x^{\prime})=H(x,x^{\prime})\,(x-x^{\prime})\,,\quad H(x,x^{\prime})\coloneqq\int_{0}^{1}\nabla^{2}V_{\eta}(x^{\prime}+t\,(x-x^{\prime}))\,\mathrm{d}t\,.] 



@S4.SS3.p2.2 By Lemma 4.1,
 [\frac{1}{2\kappa}\,I\preceq H\preceq I] .


@S4.SS3.p3 

@S4.SS3.p3.1 The first OU half-step changes  [\Delta p]  to
 [a\,\Delta p] .
Thus,





 [\Delta X_{t_{j}}^{[0]}=\Delta x+at_{j}\,\Delta p\,.] 



@S4.SS3.p3.2 Writing  [H_{j}^{[0]}\coloneqq H(X_{t_{j}}^{[0]},X_{t_{j}}^{\prime[0]})] ,
the first Picard update gives





 [\Delta X_{t_{i}}^{[1]}=\Delta x+at_{i}\,\Delta p\underbrace{-\sum_{j\in[J]}\omega_{i,j}H_{j}^{[0]}\,(\Delta x+at_{j}\,\Delta p)}_{\eqqcolon E_{i}}\,.] 



@S4.SS3.p3.3 The bounds  [\lVert H_{j}^{[0]}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant 1] ,  [a\leqslant 1] ,  [t_{j}\leqslant h] , and
 [\sum_{j\in[J]}|\omega_{i,j}|\leqslant h^{2}\Lambda_{J}/2]  (Proposition B.1) imply





 [\lVert E_{i}\rVert\leqslant\sum_{j\in[J]}|\omega_{i,j}|\,\bigl(\lVert\Delta x\rVert+at_{j}\,\lVert\Delta p\rVert\bigr)\leqslant\frac{h^{2}\Lambda_{J}}{2}\,\bigl(\lVert\Delta x\rVert+h\,\lVert\Delta p\rVert\bigr)\,.] 



@S4.SS3.p3.4 Then, set





 [H_{j}\coloneqq H\bigl(X_{t_{j}}^{[1]},X_{t_{j}}^{\prime[1]}\bigr)\,,\qquad H\coloneqq h^{-1}\sum_{j\in[J]}\omega_{j}H_{j}\,.] 



@S4.SS3.p3.5 Positivity and  [\sum_{j\in[J]}\omega_{j}=h]  imply
 [\frac{1}{2\kappa}\,I\preceq H\preceq I] . The exact endpoint differences are





 [\displaystyle\Delta X_{\rm init}^{+}] 
 [\displaystyle=\Delta x+ah\,\Delta p-\sum_{j\in[J]}\omega_{J,j}H_{j}\,\Delta X_{t_{j}}^{[1]}\,,\qquad\Delta P_{\rm init}^{+}=a^{2}\,\Delta p-a\sum_{j\in[J]}\omega_{j}H_{j}\,\Delta X_{t_{j}}^{[1]}\,.] 



@S4.SS3.p3.6 Subtract  [\Delta x+h\,\Delta p]  from the first line and
 [\Delta p-hH\,\Delta x-h\,\Delta p]  from the second. Use
 [a=1-h/2+O(h^{2})] ,  [a^{2}=1-h+O(h^{2})] ,
(B.2), and the preceding bound on  [E_{i}] . Every remaining
term is bounded by
 [C\Lambda_{J}h^{2}\,\lVert(\Delta x,\Delta p)\rVert] , which proves the claimed
expansion.
∎



@S4.Thmtheorem7 
Proposition 4.7 (Contraction).

@S4.Thmtheorem7.p1 

@S4.Thmtheorem7.p1.1 If  [h\Lambda_{J}\leqslant c/\kappa] , then, for all  [z,z^{\prime},\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}] ,





 [\lVert\Phi_{z}(\zeta)-\Phi_{z^{\prime}}(\zeta)\rVert_{M_{\kappa}}\leqslant\Bigl(1-\frac{ch}{\kappa}\Bigr)\,\lVert z-z^{\prime}\rVert_{M_{\kappa}}\,.] 

(4.13)




@S4.SS3.3 
Proof.

@S4.SS3.p4 

@S4.SS3.p4.1 Let  [q]  be a unit
eigenvector of  [H]  with eigenvalue
 [\lambda\in[\frac{1}{2\kappa},1]] . The two-dimensional phase-space subspace  [\{(uq,vq):u,v\in\mathbb{R}\}] 
is invariant under  [A_{H}] . In the coordinates  [(u,v)] , the restrictions
of  [A_{H}]  and  [M_{\kappa}]  to this subspace are represented by





 [A_{H}(uq,vq)=\bigl(vq,-(\lambda u+v)q\bigr)\,,\qquad A_{\lambda}\coloneqq\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
-\lambda&-1\end{bmatrix}\,,\qquad M_{\kappa}^{(2)}\coloneqq\begin{bmatrix}\frac{1}{2\kappa}+\frac{1}{2}&\frac{1}{2}\\[2.84526pt]
\frac{1}{2}&1\end{bmatrix}\,.] 



@S4.SS3.p4.2 A direct calculation gives





 [A_{\lambda}^{\mathsf{T}}M_{\kappa}^{(2)}+M_{\kappa}^{(2)}A_{\lambda}=-\begin{bmatrix}\lambda&\lambda-\frac{1}{2\kappa}\\
\lambda-\frac{1}{2\kappa}&1\end{bmatrix}\preceq-\frac{1}{8\kappa}\,M_{\kappa}^{(2)}\,.] 

(4.14)


@S4.SS3.p4.3 Indeed, the positive matrix after the minus sign has trace at most  [2]  and
determinant





 [\lambda-\Bigl(\lambda-\frac{1}{2\kappa}\Bigr)^{2}\geqslant\frac{1}{2\kappa}\,.] 



@S4.SS3.p4.4 Its smallest eigenvalue is therefore at least  [1/(4\kappa)] . Since
 [M_{\kappa}^{(2)}\preceq 2I] , this proves (4.14).
Decomposing along an orthonormal eigenbasis of  [H]  yields the full
phase-space inequality





 [\displaystyle A_{H}^{\mathsf{T}}M_{\kappa}+M_{\kappa}A_{H}\preceq-\frac{1}{8\kappa}\,M_{\kappa}\,.] 

(4.15)


@S4.SS3.p4.5 Apply Lemma 4.6. Uniform equivalence of
the Euclidean and  [M_{\kappa}]  norms gives





 [\lVert\mathcal{R}_{h}\Delta_{0}\rVert_{M_{\kappa}}\leqslant C\Lambda_{J}h^{2}\,\lVert\Delta_{0}\rVert_{M_{\kappa}}\,.] 



@S4.SS3.p4.6 Expanding the square and
applying (4.15) gives





 [\lVert\Delta_{1}\rVert_{M_{\kappa}}^{2}\leqslant\{1-ch/\kappa+C\Lambda_{J}h^{2}\}\,\lVert\Delta_{0}\rVert_{M_{\kappa}}^{2}\,.] 



@S4.SS3.p4.7 The step size restriction absorbs the last term. Taking square roots and changing
the universal constant gives (4.13).
∎



@S4.Thmtheorem8 
Lemma 4.8 (Synchronous increment coupling).

@S4.Thmtheorem8.p1 

@S4.Thmtheorem8.p1.1 If  [h\Lambda_{J}\leqslant c/\kappa] , then, for all  [z,z^{\prime},\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}] ,





 [\lVert\{\Phi_{z}(\zeta)-z\}-\{\Phi_{z^{\prime}}(\zeta)-z^{\prime}\}\rVert_{M_{\kappa}}\leqslant Ch\,\lVert z-z^{\prime}\rVert_{M_{\kappa}}\,.] 

(4.16)




@S4.SS3.4 
Proof.

@S4.SS3.p5 

@S4.SS3.p5.1 Subtract  [\Delta_{0}]  from the expansion in
Lemma 4.6. Uniform equivalence of the
Euclidean and  [M_{\kappa}]  norms, together with
 [\lVert H\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant 1] , gives
 [\lVert A_{H}\Delta_{0}\rVert_{M_{\kappa}}\leqslant C\,\lVert\Delta_{0}\rVert_{M_{\kappa}}] .
Moreover,
 [\Lambda_{J}h^{2}\leqslant h]  under the step-size assumption. Hence
 [\lVert\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{0}\rVert_{M_{\kappa}}\leqslant Ch\,\lVert\Delta_{0}\rVert_{M_{\kappa}}] , which is
(4.16).
∎




@S4.SS4 

4.4 Local error bounds


@S4.SS4.SSS1 

4.4.1 Bias bound


@S4.SS4.SSS1.p1 

@S4.SS4.SSS1.p1.1 We first control the displacement between the conditional mean and
exact gradient kernels. Put





 [\bar{g}_{\eta}(y)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\widehat{g}_{\eta}(y;G)=g_{\eta}(y)+b_{\eta}(y)\,.] 




@S4.Thmtheorem9 
Proposition 4.9 (Bias bound).

@S4.Thmtheorem9.p1 

@S4.Thmtheorem9.p1.1 If  [h\Lambda_{J}\leqslant 1] , then





 [\mathcal{E}_{{\rm bias}}\leqslant C\sqrt{d}\,\bigl(h\eta^{3/2}+\Lambda_{J}\eta^{1/2}h^{3}\bigr)\,.] 

(4.17)




@S4.SS4.SSS1.2 
Proof.

@S4.SS4.SSS1.p2 

@S4.SS4.SSS1.p2.1 Fix  [z,\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}]  and write





 [B\coloneqq\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\,\lVert b_{\eta}(y)\rVert\leqslant\eta^{3/2}\sqrt{d}\,,] 



@S4.SS4.SSS1.p2.2 by (4.2). To distinguish the two
first Picard updates, let  [X_{t_{i}}^{[1]}]  denote the iterate obtained with
the exact gradient  [g_{\eta}] , and let  [\widehat{X}_{t_{i}}^{[1]}]  denote
the iterate obtained with  [\widehat{g}_{\eta}] . Taking the expectation over the first
stochastic gradient block,





 [\displaystyle\bar{X}_{t_{i}}^{[1]}-X_{t_{i}}^{[1]}] 
 [\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[\widehat{X}_{t_{i}}^{[1]}\mid\zeta]-X_{t_{i}}^{[1]}=-\sum_{j\in[J]}\omega_{i,j}b_{\eta}(X_{t_{j}}^{[0]})\,.] 



@S4.SS4.SSS1.p2.3 Consequently, (B.2) gives the
estimate





 [\max_{j\in[J]}\,\lVert\bar{X}_{t_{j}}^{[1]}-X_{t_{j}}^{[1]}\rVert\leqslant\frac{h^{2}\Lambda_{J}}{2}\,B\,.] 

(4.18)


@S4.SS4.SSS1.p2.4 We also need to control the fluctuations:





 [\widehat{X}_{t_{i}}^{[1]}-\bar{X}_{t_{i}}^{[1]}=-\sum_{j\in[J]}\omega_{i,j}\xi_{\eta}(X_{t_{j}}^{[0]};G_{j}^{[0]})\,.] 



@S4.SS4.SSS1.p2.5 The variables in the sum are independent and centered, and
(4.3) implies
 [\mathbb{E}\lVert\xi_{\eta}(y;G)\rVert^{2}\leqslant\eta d] . Therefore,





 [\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\lVert\widehat{X}_{t_{i}}^{[1]}-\bar{X}_{t_{i}}^{[1]}\rVert^{2}\mid\zeta]] 
 [\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in[J]}\omega_{i,j}^{2}\,\mathbb{E}[\lVert\xi_{\eta}(X_{t_{j}}^{[0]};G_{j}^{[0]})\rVert^{2}\mid\zeta]\leqslant\eta d\,\Bigl(\sum_{j\in[J]}|\omega_{i,j}|\Bigr)^{2}\leqslant\frac{h^{4}\Lambda_{J}^{2}}{4}\,\eta d\,.] 

(4.19)



@S4.SS4.SSS1.p3 

@S4.SS4.SSS1.p3.1 It remains to propagate these errors through the endpoint update.
The endpoint stochastic gradient block is independent of the first block,
so for each  [j] ,





 [\mathbb{E}[\widehat{g}_{\eta}(\widehat{X}_{t_{j}}^{[1]};G_{j}^{[1]})\mid\zeta]=\mathbb{E}[\bar{g}_{\eta}(\widehat{X}_{t_{j}}^{[1]})\mid\zeta]\,.] 



@S4.SS4.SSS1.p3.2 Since  [\widehat{g}_{\eta}(\,\cdot\,;G)]  is  [1] -Lipschitz for every  [G] ,
so is  [\bar{g}_{\eta}] . Using
 [\bar{g}_{\eta}=g_{\eta}+b_{\eta}] , followed by
(4.18),
(4.19), and Jensen’s inequality,
we obtain





 [\displaystyle\lVert\mathbb{E}[\bar{g}_{\eta}(\widehat{X}_{t_{j}}^{[1]})\mid\zeta]-g_{\eta}(X_{t_{j}}^{[1]})\rVert] 
 [\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{E}[\lVert\widehat{X}_{t_{j}}^{[1]}-\bar{X}_{t_{j}}^{[1]}\rVert\mid\zeta]+\lVert\bar{X}_{t_{j}}^{[1]}-X_{t_{j}}^{[1]}\rVert+\lVert b_{\eta}(X_{t_{j}}^{[1]})\rVert] 





 [\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{h^{2}\Lambda_{J}}{2}\sqrt{\eta d}+\Bigl(1+\frac{h^{2}\Lambda_{J}}{2}\Bigr)\,B\,.] 




@S4.SS4.SSS1.p4 

@S4.SS4.SSS1.p4.1 The position and momentum components of
 [\bar{\Phi}_{z}(\zeta)-\Phi_{z}(\zeta)]  are obtained by weighting these
gradient differences by  [\omega_{J,j}]  and  [a\omega_{j}] , respectively.
Since





 [\sum_{j\in[J]}|\omega_{J,j}|\leqslant\frac{h^{2}\Lambda_{J}}{2}\,,\qquad a\sum_{j\in[J]}\omega_{j}=ah\leqslant h\,.] 



@S4.SS4.SSS1.p4.2 We conclude that





 [\displaystyle\lVert\bar{\Phi}_{z}(\zeta)-\Phi_{z}(\zeta)\rVert] 
 [\displaystyle\leqslant\Bigl(h+\frac{h^{2}\Lambda_{J}}{2}\Bigr)\,\Bigl\{\frac{h^{2}\Lambda_{J}}{2}\sqrt{\eta d}+\Bigl(1+\frac{h^{2}\Lambda_{J}}{2}\Bigr)B\Bigr\}] 





 [\displaystyle\leqslant Ch\,\bigl(B+h^{2}\Lambda_{J}\sqrt{\eta d}\bigr)] 





 [\displaystyle\leqslant C\sqrt{d}\,\bigl(\eta^{3/2}h+\Lambda_{J}\eta^{1/2}h^{3}\bigr)\,.] 



@S4.SS4.SSS1.p4.3 The bound is uniform in  [z]  and  [\zeta] , proving
(4.17).
∎




@S4.SS4.SSS2 

4.4.2 Variance bound


@S4.SS4.SSS2.p1 

@S4.SS4.SSS2.p1.1 Fix a phase-space input  [z\coloneqq(x_{\rm init},p_{\rm init})] , and put
 [\sigma\coloneqq(1-e^{-h})^{1/2}] . Collect the OU
variables as
 [\zeta\coloneqq(\zeta_{0},\zeta_{1})\sim\mathsf{N}(0,I_{2d})]  and all
Gaussian variables used by the stochastic gradients as  [G_{\rm all}] .
Define





 [\mathcal{G}\coloneqq h^{-1}\sum_{j\in[J]}\omega_{j}\widehat{g}_{\eta}(X_{t_{j}}^{[1]};G_{j}^{[1]})\,,\qquad\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\coloneqq h^{-2}\sum_{j\in[J]}\omega_{J,j}\widehat{g}_{\eta}(X_{t_{j}}^{[1]};G_{j}^{[1]})\,.] 



@S4.SS4.SSS2.p1.2 Direct substitution in Algorithm 3.1 gives





 [\begin{bmatrix}X_{\rm init}^{+}\\[2.5pt]
P_{\rm init}^{+}\end{bmatrix}=u+B_{h}\{\zeta+F(\zeta,G_{\rm all})\}\,,] 



@S4.SS4.SSS2.p1.3 where





 [\displaystyle u] 
 [\displaystyle\coloneqq\begin{bmatrix}x_{\rm init}+ah\,p_{\rm init}\\
a^{2}\,p_{\rm init}\end{bmatrix}\,,\qquad B_{h}\coloneqq\sigma\begin{bmatrix}hI&0\\
aI&I\end{bmatrix}\,,\qquad F\coloneqq\begin{bmatrix}-h\mathcal{G}^{\prime}/\sigma\\
ah\,(\mathcal{G}^{\prime}-\mathcal{G})/\sigma\end{bmatrix}\,.] 



@S4.SS4.SSS2.p1.4 Moreover,  [h/\sigma\asymp\sqrt{h}]  and
 [\lVert M_{\kappa}^{1/2}B_{h}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant C\sqrt{h}] .
The purpose of this decomposition is to apply the refined second-order  [W_{2}]  estimate in Lemma 4.11 below.
First, we record some Lipschitz estimates.


@S4.Thmtheorem10 
Lemma 4.10 (Lipschitz bounds).

@S4.Thmtheorem10.p1 

@S4.Thmtheorem10.p1.1 If  [h^{2}\Lambda_{J}\leqslant 1] , then, uniformly in the input,





 [\displaystyle\lVert F(\zeta,G)-F(\zeta^{\prime},G)\rVert] 
 [\displaystyle\leqslant C\Lambda_{J}h^{2}\,\lVert\zeta-\zeta^{\prime}\rVert\,,] 

(4.20)



 [\displaystyle\lVert F(\zeta,G)-F(\zeta,G^{\prime})\rVert] 
 [\displaystyle\leqslant C\Lambda_{J}\sqrt{\eta h}\,\lVert G-G^{\prime}\rVert\,.] 





@S4.SS4.SSS2.2 
Proof.

@S4.SS4.SSS2.p2 

@S4.SS4.SSS2.p2.1 Consider first two OU inputs  [\zeta\coloneqq(\zeta_{0},\zeta_{1})]  and
 [\zeta^{\prime}\coloneqq(\zeta_{0}^{\prime},\zeta_{1}^{\prime})] , with the stochastic gradient variables
fixed. The map  [F]  does not depend on  [\zeta_{1}] . Moreover,





 [\lVert X_{t_{j}}^{[0]}(\zeta_{0})-X_{t_{j}}^{[0]}(\zeta_{0}^{\prime})\rVert=\sigma t_{j}\,\lVert\zeta_{0}-\zeta_{0}^{\prime}\rVert\leqslant\sigma h\,\lVert\zeta_{0}-\zeta_{0}^{\prime}\rVert\,.] 



@S4.SS4.SSS2.p2.2 Using (4.4) and then (B.2) gives






 [\displaystyle\max_{j\in[J]}\,\lVert X_{t_{j}}^{[1]}(\zeta_{0},G^{[0]})-X_{t_{j}}^{[1]}(\zeta_{0}^{\prime},G^{[0]})\rVert\leqslant\sigma h\,\lVert\zeta_{0}-\zeta_{0}^{\prime}\rVert+\frac{h^{2}\Lambda_{J}}{2}\,\sigma h\,\lVert\zeta_{0}-\zeta_{0}^{\prime}\rVert\leqslant 2\sigma h\,\lVert\zeta_{0}-\zeta_{0}^{\prime}\rVert\,.] 



@S4.SS4.SSS2.p2.3 Since  [\omega_{j}\geqslant 0] ,  [\sum_{j\in[J]}\omega_{j}=h] , and
 [\sum_{j\in[J]}|\omega_{J,j}|\leqslant h^{2}\Lambda_{J}/2] , it follows that





 [\lVert\Delta\mathcal{G}\rVert\leqslant C\sigma h\,\lVert\Delta\zeta_{0}\rVert\,,\qquad\lVert\Delta\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\rVert\leqslant C\Lambda_{J}\sigma h\,\lVert\Delta\zeta_{0}\rVert\,.] 



@S4.SS4.SSS2.p2.4 Multiplying by the factor  [h/\sigma]  in the definition of  [F]  proves
(4.20).


@S4.SS4.SSS2.p3 

@S4.SS4.SSS2.p3.1 Now fix  [\zeta]  and compare two collections
 [G\coloneqq(G^{[0]},G^{[1]})]  and
 [G^{\prime}\coloneqq(G^{\prime[0]},G^{\prime[1]})] . By (4.5),





 [\max_{j\in[J]}\,\lVert\Delta X_{t_{j}}^{[1]}\rVert\leqslant\frac{h^{2}\Lambda_{J}}{2}\sqrt{\eta}\,\lVert G^{[0]}-G^{\prime[0]}\rVert\,.] 



@S4.SS4.SSS2.p3.2 At the endpoint gradient evaluations, the first block changes the input
locations through  [\Delta X_{t_{j}}^{[1]}] , whereas the second block
changes the Gaussian arguments directly. The Lipschitz estimates in both
arguments therefore yield





 [\displaystyle\lVert\Delta\mathcal{G}\rVert] 
 [\displaystyle\leqslant\sqrt{\eta}\,\bigl\{\Lambda_{J}h^{2}\,\lVert\Delta G^{[0]}\rVert+\lVert\Delta G^{[1]}\rVert\bigr\}\,,\qquad\lVert\Delta\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\rVert\leqslant\sqrt{\eta}\,\bigl\{\Lambda_{J}^{2}h^{2}\,\lVert\Delta G^{[0]}\rVert+\Lambda_{J}\,\lVert\Delta G^{[1]}\rVert\bigr\}\,.] 



@S4.SS4.SSS2.p3.3 Use  [h/\sigma\leqslant C\sqrt{h}]  in the definition of  [F] ,
together with  [h^{2}\Lambda_{J}\leqslant 1] , to obtain the second bound.
∎



@S4.SS4.SSS2.p4 

@S4.SS4.SSS2.p4.1 The key to our analysis is the following refined bound.


@S4.Thmtheorem11 
Lemma 4.11 (Second-order  [W_{2}]  estimate).

@S4.Thmtheorem11.p1 

@S4.Thmtheorem11.p1.1 Let  [\mathcal{H}]  be a Hilbert space, let  [Z\sim\mathsf{N}(0,I_{m})]  and
 [G\sim\mathsf{N}(0,I_{n})]  be independent, and let
 [f:\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}]  satisfy





 [\lVert f(z,g)-f(z^{\prime},g)\rVert\leqslant A\,\lVert z-z^{\prime}\rVert\,,\qquad\lVert f(z,g)-f(z,g^{\prime})\rVert\leqslant B\,\lVert g-g^{\prime}\rVert\,.] 



@S4.Thmtheorem11.p1.2 Assume that  [f(z,g)-f(z,g^{\prime})]  belongs to a fixed subspace
 [E\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}] , and let  [\Pi_{E}]  denote the orthogonal projection
onto  [E] . If  [L:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathcal{H}]  is linear and
 [\bar{f}(Z)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[f(Z,G)\mid Z]] , then





 [W_{2}\bigl(\law\{L(Z+f(Z,G))\},\law\{L(Z+\bar{f}(Z))\}\bigr)\leqslant\sqrt{m}\,\Bigl\{\frac{1}{2}\,\lVert L\Pi_{E}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\,B^{2}+5\,\lVert L\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\,AB\Bigr\}\,.] 

(4.21)




@S4.SS4.SSS2.p5 

@S4.SS4.SSS2.p5.1 It is instructive to compare this to a more trivial bound.
The obvious coupling, together with the Gaussian Poincaré inequality applied conditionally on  [Z]  and over the subspace  [E] , yields





 [\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\lVert L(Z+f(Z,G))-L(Z+\bar{f}(Z))\rVert^{2}]] 
 [\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\lVert L\Pi_{E}(f(Z,G)-\bar{f}(Z))\rVert^{2}]] 





 [\displaystyle\leqslant\lVert L\Pi_{E}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\,\mathbb{E}[\lVert f(Z,G)-\bar{f}(Z)\rVert^{2}]\leqslant m\,\lVert L\Pi_{E}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\,B^{2}\,.] 



@S4.SS4.SSS2.p5.2 This yields





 [W_{2}\bigl(\law\{L(Z+f(Z,G))\},\law\{L(Z+\bar{f}(Z))\}\bigr)\leqslant\sqrt{m}\,\lVert L\Pi_{E}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\,B\,.] 



@S4.SS4.SSS2.p5.3 This argument does not use any assumption on the distribution of  [Z] .
In contrast, Lemma 4.11 uses the fact that  [Z]  is Gaussian, together with the fact that the difference  [f(Z,G)-\bar{f}(Z)]  is conditionally centered, to obtain a second-order bound which depends quadratically on  [A]  and  [B] .
The proof, given in
Section C.3, is based on heat flow interpolation and Gaussian integration by parts.
We remark that for the purpose of this paper, we only need the case  [E=\mathbb{R}^{m}] .


@S4.Thmtheorem12 
Remark 4.12.

@S4.Thmtheorem12.p1 

@S4.Thmtheorem12.p1.1 Related second-order estimates are known for centered perturbations
independent of the Gaussian variable being perturbed: see
[59, Lemma 1] for bounded perturbations on a one-dimensional
subspace, [52, Theorems 2.1 and 2.7] for more general Gaussian
convolution inequalities, and the Ingster–Suslina second moment method [32] for divergence bounds.
A complementary heat flow perspective is given by
[18, Theorem 2.1]: the first unmatched moment determines the
asymptotic decay of  [W_{2}]  under Gaussian smoothing.
These independent perturbation results do not apply directly when
 [f(Z,G)-\bar{f}(Z)]  depends on the Gaussian  [Z]  providing the
smoothing; the  [AB]  term in Lemma 4.11
accounts for this dependence.



@S4.SS4.SSS2.p6 

@S4.SS4.SSS2.p6.1 With this in hand, we can now bound the variance term.


@S4.Thmtheorem13 
Proposition 4.13 (Variance bound).

@S4.Thmtheorem13.p1 

@S4.Thmtheorem13.p1.1 Under  [h^{2}\Lambda_{J}\leqslant 1] ,





 [\mathcal{E}_{{\rm var}}\leqslant C\Lambda_{J}^{2}\sqrt{d}\,\bigl(\eta h^{3/2}+\eta^{1/2}h^{3}\bigr)\,.] 





@S4.SS4.SSS2.3 
Proof.

@S4.SS4.SSS2.p7 

@S4.SS4.SSS2.p7.1 Apply Lemma 4.11 with





 [m=2d\,,\qquad Z=\zeta\,,\qquad G=G_{\rm all}\,,\qquad f=F\,,\qquad L=B_{h}\,,\qquad E=\mathbb{R}^{2d}\,.] 



@S4.SS4.SSS2.p7.2 By Lemma 4.10, we may take





 [A=C\Lambda_{J}h^{2}\qquad\text{and}\qquad B=C\Lambda_{J}\sqrt{\eta h}\,.] 



@S4.SS4.SSS2.p7.3 Since  [\lVert B_{h}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant C\sqrt{h}] ,
Eq. 4.21 yields





 [\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{{\rm var}}] 
 [\displaystyle\leqslant C\sqrt{d}\,\lVert B_{h}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\,(B^{2}+AB)\leqslant C\Lambda_{J}^{2}\sqrt{d}\,(\eta h^{3/2}+\eta^{1/2}h^{3})\,.\qed] 






@S4.SS4.SSS3 

4.4.3 Discretization error bound


@S4.SS4.SSS3.p1 

@S4.SS4.SSS3.p1.1 Consider the Hamiltonian generator.





 [\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{H}}\coloneqq\langle p,\nabla_{x}\rangle-\langle g_{\eta}(x),\nabla_{p}\rangle] 



@S4.SS4.SSS3.p1.2 For a position vector field  [W=(W_{i})_{i\in[d]}] , put
 [\delta_{\pi_{\eta}}W\coloneqq\langle g_{\eta},W\rangle-\operatorname{div}_{x}W] . Integration by
parts under  [e^{-V_{\eta}}]  gives the identity





 [\lVert\delta_{\pi_{\eta}}W\rVert_{L^{2}(\pi_{\eta})}^{2}\leqslant\lVert W\rVert_{L^{2}(\pi_{\eta};\mathrm{HS})}^{2}+\lVert D_{x}W\rVert_{L^{2}(\pi_{\eta};\mathrm{HS})}^{2}\,.] 

(4.22)


@S4.SS4.SSS3.p1.3 Indeed, expanding the square and integrating the two cross terms produces a
contraction of the derivative arrays and the quadratic form generated by
 [\nabla^{2}V_{\eta}] ; Cauchy–Schwarz and
 [0\preceq\nabla^{2}V_{\eta}\preceq I]  give the displayed bound.


@S4.SS4.SSS3.p2 

@S4.SS4.SSS3.p2.1 The following lemma relies on Gaussian polynomial bounds collected in
Lemma C.7. In particular, if a
Hilbert-valued momentum polynomial  [F(p)]  and a polynomial vector field
 [W(p)]  have degree at most  [r] , then





 [\lVert D_{p}F\rVert_{L^{2}(\gamma;\mathrm{HS})}\leqslant\sqrt{r}\,\lVert F\rVert_{L^{2}(\gamma)}\,,\qquad\lVert\delta_{\gamma}W\rVert_{L^{2}(\gamma)}\leqslant\sqrt{r+1}\,\lVert W\rVert_{L^{2}(\gamma;\mathrm{HS})}\,.] 

(4.23)



@S4.Thmtheorem14 
Lemma 4.14 (Stationary iterated derivatives in  [L^{2}] ).

@S4.Thmtheorem14.p1 

@S4.Thmtheorem14.p1.1 For every integer  [k\geqslant 0] ,





 [\lVert\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{H}}^{k}g_{\eta}\rVert_{L^{2}(\Pi_{\eta})}\leqslant C^{k+1}(k!)^{3/2}\sqrt{d}\,\eta^{-k/2}\,.] 





@S4.SS4.SSS3.2 
Proof.

@S4.SS4.SSS3.p3 

@S4.SS4.SSS3.p3.1 For a tensor-valued function  [F] , define the weighted Sobolev norm





 [\lVert F\rVert_{\mathsf{W}^{N,2}}\coloneqq\sum_{j=0}^{N}\frac{\eta^{j/2}}{j!}\,\lVert D_{x}^{j}F\rVert_{L^{2}(\Pi_{\eta};\mathrm{HS})}\,.] 



@S4.SS4.SSS3.p3.2 The weight ratio gives
 [\lVert D_{x}F\rVert_{\mathsf{W}^{N-1,2}}\leqslant N\eta^{-1/2}\,\lVert F\rVert_{\mathsf{W}^{N,2}}] . We next establish the
corresponding bound for  [\delta_{\pi_{\eta}}] . For  [0\leqslant j\leqslant N-1] , the
notation





 [\operatorname{contr}(D_{x}^{\ell}g_{\eta},D_{x}^{j-\ell}W)\coloneqq\operatorname{Sym}\Bigl(\sum_{a\in[d]}D_{x}^{\ell}(g_{\eta})_{a}\otimes D_{x}^{j-\ell}W_{a}\Bigr)] 



@S4.SS4.SSS3.p3.3 denotes contraction over the position index  [a] , followed by
normalized symmetrization over the  [j]  derivative indices. The
higher-order Leibniz rule [51, Exercise 1.2.13] gives





 [D_{x}^{j}(\delta_{\pi_{\eta}}W)=\delta_{\pi_{\eta}}(D_{x}^{j}W)+\sum_{\ell=1}^{j}\binom{j}{\ell}\operatorname{contr}(D_{x}^{\ell}g_{\eta},D_{x}^{j-\ell}W)\,.] 



@S4.SS4.SSS3.p3.4 By (4.22), the weighted sum of the first terms is
bounded by





 [\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\frac{\eta^{j/2}}{j!}\,\lVert\delta_{\pi_{\eta}}(D_{x}^{j}W)\rVert_{L^{2}(\Pi_{\eta})}] 
 [\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\frac{\eta^{j/2}}{j!}\,\bigl(\lVert D_{x}^{j}W\rVert_{L^{2}(\Pi_{\eta};\mathrm{HS})}+\lVert D_{x}^{j+1}W\rVert_{L^{2}(\Pi_{\eta};\mathrm{HS})}\bigr)] 





 [\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{2N}{\sqrt{\eta}}\,\lVert W\rVert_{\mathsf{W}^{N,2}}\,.] 



@S4.SS4.SSS3.p3.5 For the remaining terms, (4.8) implies





 [\lVert\operatorname{contr}(D_{x}^{\ell}g_{\eta},D_{x}^{j-\ell}W)\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}\leqslant\ell!\,\eta^{-(\ell-1)/2}\,\lVert D_{x}^{j-\ell}W\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}\,.] 



@S4.SS4.SSS3.p3.6 Relative to the weight of  [D_{x}^{j-\ell}W] , the coefficient of the term
with  [\ell]  derivatives on  [g_{\eta}]  is





 [\frac{\eta^{j/2}}{j!}\,\binom{j}{\ell}\,\ell!\,\eta^{-(\ell-1)/2}\bigg/\frac{\eta^{(j-\ell)/2}}{(j-\ell)!}=\sqrt{\eta}\,.] 



@S4.SS4.SSS3.p3.7 After setting  [s=j-\ell] , each fixed position derivative  [D_{x}^{s}W] 
appears for at most  [N-1-s\leqslant N]  choices of  [\ell] . Thus, the
commutator contribution is at most
 [N\sqrt{\eta}\,\lVert W\rVert_{\mathsf{W}^{N,2}}] .
Combining the two contributions, using  [0<\eta\leqslant 1]  and  [N\geqslant 1] , yields





 [\lVert\delta_{\pi_{\eta}}W\rVert_{\mathsf{W}^{N-1,2}}\leqslant\frac{CN}{\sqrt{\eta}}\,\lVert W\rVert_{\mathsf{W}^{N,2}}\,.] 



@S4.SS4.SSS3.p3.8 It remains to control the Hamiltonian generator. One can check that





 [\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{H}}F=\delta_{\gamma}(D_{x}F)-\delta_{\pi_{\eta}}(D_{p}F)\,.] 

(4.24)


@S4.SS4.SSS3.p3.9 Suppose that  [F]  has momentum
degree at most  [r] . Since  [D_{x}]  commutes with  [\delta_{\gamma}] , the
Gaussian divergence estimate in (4.23) gives





 [\displaystyle\lVert\delta_{\gamma}(D_{x}F)\rVert_{\mathsf{W}^{N-1,2}}] 
 [\displaystyle\leqslant\sqrt{r+1}\,\lVert D_{x}F\rVert_{\mathsf{W}^{N-1,2}}\leqslant\frac{N\sqrt{r+1}}{\sqrt{\eta}}\,\lVert F\rVert_{\mathsf{W}^{N,2}}\,.] 



@S4.SS4.SSS3.p3.10 For the second term, the Sobolev divergence estimate above and the Gaussian
gradient estimate in (4.23) yield





 [\displaystyle\lVert\delta_{\pi_{\eta}}(D_{p}F)\rVert_{\mathsf{W}^{N-1,2}}] 
 [\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{CN}{\sqrt{\eta}}\,\lVert D_{p}F\rVert_{\mathsf{W}^{N,2}}\leqslant\frac{CN\sqrt{r}}{\sqrt{\eta}}\,\lVert F\rVert_{\mathsf{W}^{N,2}}\,.] 



@S4.SS4.SSS3.p3.11 Combining the last two displays proves





 [\displaystyle\lVert\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{H}}F\rVert_{\mathsf{W}^{N-1,2}}\leqslant\frac{CN\sqrt{r+1}}{\sqrt{\eta}}\,\lVert F\rVert_{\mathsf{W}^{N,2}}\,.] 

(4.25)


@S4.SS4.SSS3.p3.12 Finally, integration by parts gives
 [\lVert g_{\eta}\rVert_{L^{2}(\pi_{\eta})}^{2}=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{\eta}}\Delta V_{\eta}\leqslant d] , while
(4.7) gives





 [\lVert g_{\eta}\rVert_{\mathsf{W}^{N,2}}\leqslant\sqrt{d}+N\sqrt{\eta d}\leqslant(N+1)\sqrt{d}\,.] 



@S4.SS4.SSS3.p3.13 Since
 [\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{H}}^{j}g_{\eta}]  has momentum degree at most  [j] , iterating the
bound (4.25) for  [j=0,\ldots,k-1]  produces
 [k!\sqrt{k!}] . The extra factor  [k+1]  from the initialization is
absorbed into  [C^{k+1}] , proving the claim.
∎



@S4.SS4.SSS3.p4 

@S4.SS4.SSS3.p4.1 Let  [(X_{t},P_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}]  be the exact Hamiltonian flow and put
 [g(t)\coloneqq g_{\eta}(X_{t})]  when the initial state has law  [\Pi_{\eta}] . Invariance
and Lemma 4.14 give





 [\sup_{0\leqslant t\leqslant h}\,\lVert g^{(k)}(t)\rVert_{L^{2}}\leqslant C^{k+1}(k!)^{3/2}\sqrt{d}\,\eta^{-k/2}\,.] 

(4.26)



@S4.Thmtheorem15 
Proposition 4.15 (Discretization error bound).

@S4.Thmtheorem15.p1 

@S4.Thmtheorem15.p1.1 If  [h^{2}\Lambda_{J}\leqslant 1] , then





 [\mathcal{E}_{{\rm disc}}\leqslant C\sqrt{d}\,\Bigl[B_{J,2}h\,\Bigl(\frac{h}{\sqrt{\eta}}\Bigr)^{J}+\Lambda_{J}^{2}h^{5}\Bigr]\,.] 

(4.27)


@S4.Thmtheorem15.p1.2 Here, one may take
 [B_{J,2}=C_{0}^{J+1}\sqrt{J!}] , where  [C_{0}]  is universal.



@S4.SS4.SSS3.3 
Proof.

@S4.SS4.SSS3.p5 

@S4.SS4.SSS3.p5.1 For an  [L^{2}(\Pi_{\eta};\mathbb{R}^{d})] -valued curve  [f]  on  [[0,h]] , write
