 [\displaystyle\mathrm{d}P_{t}] 
 [\displaystyle=-\Bigl\{\frac{X_{t}-y}{a}+\nabla V(x)+\gamma P_{t}\Bigr\}\,\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2\gamma}\,\mathrm{d}B_{t}\,,\qquad c_{0}a^{-1/2}\leqslant\gamma\leqslant C_{0}a^{-1/2}\,.] 

(A.1)


@A1.SS2.p1.3 The full algorithm is described below.


@A1.Thmtheorem3 
Algorithm A.3 (FORS for RGO).

@A1.Thmtheorem3.p1 

@A1.Thmtheorem3.p1.1 Fix a block length  [T>0] , a number  [N]  of blocks, a mesh width
 [h_{\rm mesh}]  dividing  [T] , a clipping level  [B_{\rm clip}>0] , and a
stopping set  [\mathcal{E}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}] . Compute
 [x^{+}\coloneqq\operatorname{prox}_{aV}(y)] . On each attempt, initialize





 [X^{0}\sim\mathsf{N}\Bigl(x^{+},\frac{a}{1+a\beta}I\Bigr)\qquad\text{and}\qquad P^{0}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,I)] 



@A1.Thmtheorem3.p1.2 independently. Restart the attempt if  [(X^{0},P^{0})\notin\mathcal{E}] .


@A1.Thmtheorem3.p2 

@A1.Thmtheorem3.p2.1 For  [n=0,\ldots,N-1] , repeat the following proposal until it is accepted.
Starting from  [x=X^{n}]  and  [p=P^{n}] , draw
 [M\sim\mathsf{Poisson}(2B_{\rm clip})]  and independent
 [t_{j}\sim\mathsf{Unif}[0,T)] ,  [j\in[M]] . Jointly sample the linear
proposal (A.1), its driving Brownian motion, and all
values needed below. Let  [r_{j}\in[T/h_{\rm mesh}]]  be the unique index such
that  [(r_{j}-1)h_{\rm mesh}\leqslant t_{j}<r_{j}h_{\rm mesh}] , and set





 [\displaystyle\mu_{t}] 
 [\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{\nabla V(X_{t})-\nabla V(x)}{\sqrt{2\gamma}}\,,] 




 [\displaystyle-\mathcal{W}(t_{j})] 
 [\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{T}{\sqrt{2\gamma}\,h_{\rm mesh}}\,\langle B_{T}-B_{r_{j}h_{\rm mesh}},\nabla V(X_{r_{j}h_{\rm mesh}})-\nabla V(X_{(r_{j}-1)h_{\rm mesh}})\rangle+\frac{T}{2}\,\lVert\mu_{t_{j}}\rVert^{2}\,.] 



@A1.Thmtheorem3.p2.2 Accept the proposed endpoint  [(X_{T},P_{T})]  with probability





 [\prod_{j\in[M]}\frac{B_{\rm clip}+\operatorname{clip}_{[-B_{\rm clip},B_{\rm clip}]}(\mathcal{W}(t_{j}))}{2B_{\rm clip}}\,.] 



@A1.Thmtheorem3.p2.3 Upon acceptance, set  [(X^{n+1},P^{n+1})\coloneqq(X_{T},P_{T})] . If the accepted
state lies outside  [\mathcal{E}] , restart from the initialization step.
After  [N]  accepted blocks, return  [X^{N}] .



@A1.Thmtheorem4 
Theorem A.4 (FORS for RGO distributions).

@A1.Thmtheorem4.p1 

@A1.Thmtheorem4.p1.1 Let  [V:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}]  be convex and  [\beta] -smooth, and let
 [R_{a,y}]  be the law above.
Fix  [q\geqslant 2]  and  [0<\varepsilon,p\leqslant 1/2] .

1. 

@A1.I1.i1.p1 

@A1.I1.i1.p1.1 If  [0<a\beta\leqslant 1] , there is a randomized routine using evaluations of
 [\nabla V]  and one evaluation of  [\operatorname{prox}_{aV}(y)]  whose output law
 [\widehat{R}_{a,y}]  satisfies





 [\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathsf{R}_{q}(\widehat{R}_{a,y}\mathbin{\|}R_{a,y})\leqslant\varepsilon^{2}\,.] 

(A.2)


@A1.I1.i1.p1.2 Writing





 [\mathfrak{L}\coloneqq q+\log\frac{Cd}{a\beta\varepsilon p}\,,] 



@A1.I1.i1.p1.3 its expected number of queries and, with probability at least  [1-p] , its
realized number of queries are at most





 [C\mathfrak{L}^{5/3}\,\bigl\{1+\bigl((a\beta)^{2}d\bigr)^{1/3}\bigr\}\,.] 

(A.3)


2. 

@A1.I1.i2.p1 

@A1.I1.i2.p1.1 Suppose that a
point  [x_{+}]  is supplied such that





 [\lVert y-a\nabla V(x_{+})-x_{+}\rVert\leqslant\sqrt{da}\,.] 



@A1.I1.i2.p1.2 If





 [a^{-1}\geqslant C\beta\,\bigl[\sqrt{d\,(q+\log(1/\varepsilon))}+q+\log(1/\varepsilon)\bigr]\,,] 



@A1.I1.i2.p1.3 then a gradient-only routine returns a law satisfying
(A.2). It uses  [O(1)]  expected evaluations
of  [\nabla V] , and at most  [C\log(2/p)]  evaluations with probability at
least  [1-p] .





@A1.SS2.2 
Proof.

@A1.SS2.p2 

@A1.SS2.p2.1 The second statement is the smooth case of
[15, Theorem D.1]. We therefore focus on the first statement, which is based on Algorithm A.3.


@A1.SS2.p3 

@A1.SS2.p3.1 By rescaling, it suffices to consider  [\beta=1] .
Let
 [x^{+}\coloneqq\operatorname{prox}_{aV}(y)] , set  [x=x^{+}+\sqrt{a}\,z] , and define





 [F(z)\coloneqq V(x^{+}+\sqrt{a}\,z)-V(x^{+})-\sqrt{a}\,\langle\nabla V(x^{+}),z\rangle\,.] 



@A1.SS2.p3.2 After a change of variables, we consider the density proportional to





 [z\mapsto\exp\Bigl\{-\frac{1}{2}\,\lVert z\rVert^{2}-F(z)\Bigr\}\,,\qquad F(0)=0\,,\qquad\nabla F(0)=0\,,\qquad 0\preceq\nabla^{2}F\preceq aI\,.] 



@A1.SS2.p3.3 Writing  [g\coloneqq\nabla F]  and
 [\bar{\gamma}\coloneqq\sqrt{a}\,\gamma] , the corresponding space–time rescaling
transforms (A.1), conditionally on the blockwise
initial state  [(Z_{0},P_{0})=(z,p)] , into





 [\displaystyle\mathrm{d}Z_{t}] 
 [\displaystyle=P_{t}\,\mathrm{d}t\,,] 




 [\displaystyle\mathrm{d}P_{t}] 
 [\displaystyle=-\{Z_{t}+g(z)+\bar{\gamma}P_{t}\}\,\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2\bar{\gamma}}\,\mathrm{d}B_{t}\,,\qquad c_{0}\leqslant\bar{\gamma}\leqslant C_{0}\,.] 

(A.4)



@A1.SS2.p4 

@A1.SS2.p4.1 We explain the only changes needed in the FORS proof of [17]. Let
 [S]  solve





 [S^{\prime\prime}+\bar{\gamma}S^{\prime}+S=0\,,\qquad S(0)=0\,,\qquad S^{\prime}(0)=1\,,\qquad b_{t}\coloneqq\int_{0}^{t}S_{r}\,\mathrm{d}r\,.] 



@A1.SS2.p4.2 For bounded  [\bar{\gamma}]  and sufficiently small  [t] ,
 [|S_{t}|\leqslant Ct] ,  [|b_{t}|\leqslant Ct^{2}] , and
 [|S_{t}^{\prime}|+|S_{t}^{\prime\prime}|\leqslant C] . Under
(A.4),





 [Z_{t}-z=S_{t}\,p-b_{t}\,\{z+g(z)\}+\sqrt{2\bar{\gamma}}\int_{0}^{t}S_{t-s}\,\mathrm{d}B_{s}\,.] 

(A.5)


@A1.SS2.p4.3 Consequently the Girsanov control contains only the residual force,





 [\mu_{t}\coloneqq\frac{g(Z_{t})-g(z)}{\sqrt{2\bar{\gamma}}}\,.] 



@A1.SS2.p4.4 This is the main improvement due to exact integration of the linear term.


@A1.SS2.p5 

@A1.SS2.p5.1 Now put





 [\mathcal{R}(z,p)\coloneqq\lVert p\rVert^{2}+\lVert z\rVert^{2}+a^{-1}\,\lVert g(z)\rVert^{2}+d\,.] 



@A1.SS2.p5.2 The calculations in
[17, Lemmas B.1 and B.3], applied to
(A.5), gives the same estimate
as in that paper with its full smoothness replaced by the residual
smoothness  [a] . In particular, the random likelihood factor has
sub-Gaussian scale





 [\log\mathbb{E}\exp\{\lambda\,|\mathcal{W}_{t}|\}\leqslant\log 2+C\lambda^{2}\,\frac{a^{2}T^{3}}{\bar{\gamma}}\,\mathcal{R}(z,p)\,,\qquad 1\leqslant\lambda\leqslant\frac{c}{aT^{2}}\,.] 

(A.6)


@A1.SS2.p5.3 If the control is frozen on a mesh of width  [h_{\rm mesh}] , its
discretization error  [\mathcal{E}_{\rm mesh}]  satisfies





 [\log\mathbb{E}\exp\{\lambda\,|\mathcal{E}_{\rm mesh}|\}\leqslant C\lambda\,\frac{ah_{\rm mesh}}{\bar{\gamma}}\,(d^{-1/2}+aT^{2})\,\mathcal{R}(z,p)\,.] 

(A.7)


@A1.SS2.p5.4 The proofs are the same as
[17, Lemma C.1 and the proof of Proposition 3.1]; the bounds on
 [S_{t}]  above replace the exponential Euler bounds there.


@A1.SS2.p6 

@A1.SS2.p6.1 Applying Lemma A.1 using
Eqs. A.6 and A.7, and then choosing the
clipping level and mesh exactly as in that proof, gives the required
error on one block. The stopped composition and query tail arguments
are unchanged from [17, Theorem B.11]. One  [\mathcal{W}_{t}]  factor
uses  [g]  at no more than three times. Since the linear process
 [(Z,P,B)]  can be sampled by Gaussian regression at only those times and the
two adjacent mesh points, refining the mesh makes no additional
gradient queries.


@A1.SS2.p7 

@A1.SS2.p7.1 Initialize from  [\mathsf{N}(0,(1+a)^{-1}\,I)\otimes\mathsf{N}(0,I)] . The bounds





 [\frac{1}{2}\,\lVert z\rVert^{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\,\lVert z\rVert^{2}+F(z)\leqslant\frac{1+a}{2}\,\lVert z\rVert^{2}] 



@A1.SS2.p7.2 imply that the initial
 [\mathsf{R}_{2q}]  divergence is at most  [ad/2] . For the choices below,
the logarithmic factor in the stopped composition argument satisfies  [2q+\log\frac{CNq}{\varepsilon^{2}}\leqslant C\mathfrak{L}] .
Take





 [T\coloneqq c\mathfrak{L}^{-2/3}\min\{1,(a^{2}d)^{-1/3}\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad N\coloneqq\bigl\lceil C\mathfrak{L}/T\bigr\rceil\,.] 



@A1.SS2.p7.3 Indeed, the cubic restriction from
Eq. A.6 is
 [T^{3}\leqslant c/(a^{2}d\mathfrak{L}^{2})] , while the quadratic restriction is
 [T^{2}\leqslant c/(a\mathfrak{L})] ; the displayed choice satisfies both.
The mesh is then refined until
Eq. A.7 gives the required path accuracy, without
additional gradient queries. Since the target is  [1] -strongly
log-concave, a total time  [NT\geqslant C\mathfrak{L}]  suffices in the
LSI mixing and Rényi composition argument of
[17, Theorem 3.2(ii)], run at Rényi order  [2q] . This yields
(A.2). Moreover,





 [N\leqslant C\mathfrak{L}^{5/3}\,\{1+(a^{2}d)^{1/3}\}\,.] 



@A1.SS2.p7.4 The stopped implementation uses  [O(N)]  expected queries and
 [O(N+\log(1/p))]  queries with probability at least  [1-p] . Since
 [\log(1/p)\leqslant\mathfrak{L}] , this proves
(A.3).
∎





@A2 

Appendix B Chebyshev–Lobatto interpolation and quadrature


@A2.p1 

@A2.p1.1 This appendix collects the interpolation facts used by the Picard
integrator.


@A2.SS1 

B.1 Nodes, interpolating polynomials, and integrated weights


@A2.SS1.p1 

@A2.SS1.p1.1 For an integer  [J\geqslant 2]  indicating the total number of nodes, define the increasing Chebyshev–Lobatto nodes on
 [[0,h]]  by





 [t_{j}\coloneqq\frac{h}{2}\,\bigl\{1-\cos\bigl({\textstyle\frac{j-1}{J-1}}\,\uppi\bigr)\bigr\}\,,\qquad 1\leqslant j\leqslant J\,.] 



@A2.SS1.p1.2 Their interpolating polynomials are





 [\ell_{j}(t)\coloneqq\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leqslant k\leqslant J\\
k\neq j\end{subarray}}\frac{t-t_{k}}{t_{j}-t_{k}}\,,\qquad\ell_{j}(t_{k})=\mathbf{1}_{\{j=k\}}\,.] 



@A2.SS1.p1.3 Thus, the operator  [\mathcal{I}_{J,h}]  in
(3.5) is the unique polynomial interpolant of
degree at most  [J-1] . Its Lebesgue constant is defined as





 [\Lambda_{J}\coloneqq\sup_{0\leqslant t\leqslant h}\sum_{j=1}^{J}|\ell_{j}(t)|\,.] 

(B.1)


@A2.SS1.p1.4 For a normed space  [E]  and  [\{f(t_{j})\}_{j=1}^{J}\subseteq E] , the triangle
inequality immediately gives





 [\sup_{0\leqslant t\leqslant h}\,\lVert(\mathcal{I}_{J,h}f)(t)\rVert_{E}\leqslant\Lambda_{J}\max_{j\in[J]}\,\lVert f(t_{j})\rVert_{E}\,.] 




@A2.SS1.p2 

@A2.SS1.p2.1 Recall the quadrature coefficients  [\omega_{i,j}]  and  [\omega_{j}]  from
(3.6). By their
definition, for every polynomial  [f]  of degree at most  [J-1] ,





 [\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t_{i}}(t_{i}-t)f(t)\,\mathrm{d}t] 
 [\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\omega_{i,j}f(t_{j})\,,\qquad\int_{0}^{h}f(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\omega_{j}f(t_{j})\,.] 




@A2.Thmtheorem1 
Proposition B.1 (Chebyshev–Lobatto coefficient bounds).

@A2.Thmtheorem1.p1 

@A2.Thmtheorem1.p1.2 For every  [J\geqslant 2] ,



@A2.E2X 

 [\displaystyle\max_{i\in[J]}\sum_{j\in[J]}|\omega_{i,j}|] 
 [\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{h^{2}}{2}\,\Lambda_{J}\,,] 
 [\displaystyle\omega_{j}] 
 [\displaystyle\geqslant 0\,,] 
 [\displaystyle\sum_{j\in[J]}\omega_{j}] 
 [\displaystyle=h\,,] 
 [\displaystyle\Lambda_{J}] 
 [\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{2}{\uppi}\log J+1\,.] 

(B.2)




@A2.SS1.2 
Proof.

@A2.SS1.p3 

@A2.SS1.p3.1 The classical estimate for  [\Lambda_{J}]  can be found in [60, Theorem 15.2]. Using
(B.1) under the defining integrals gives





 [\displaystyle\sum_{j\in[J]}|\omega_{i,j}|] 
 [\displaystyle\leqslant\int_{0}^{t_{i}}(t_{i}-t)\sum_{j\in[J]}|\ell_{j}(t)|\,\mathrm{d}t\leqslant\frac{t_{i}^{2}}{2}\,\Lambda_{J}\leqslant\frac{h^{2}}{2}\,\Lambda_{J}\,.] 



@A2.SS1.p3.2 The  [\omega_{j}] ’s are the
Clenshaw–Curtis weights, which are non-negative. Since the rule integrates
constants exactly on  [[0,h]] , they sum to  [h] .
∎




@A2.SS2 

B.2 Interpolation error bound


@A2.SS2.p1 

@A2.SS2.p1.1 We record the standard interpolation error bound.


@A2.Thmtheorem2 
Lemma B.2 (Banach-valued interpolation error bound).

@A2.Thmtheorem2.p1 

@A2.Thmtheorem2.p1.1 Let  [E]  be a Banach space and let  [f\in C^{J}([0,h];E)] . Then





 [\lVert f(t)-(\mathcal{I}_{J,h}f)(t)\rVert_{E}\leqslant\frac{1}{J!}\sup_{0\leqslant s\leqslant h}\,\lVert f^{(J)}(s)\rVert_{E}\prod_{j=1}^{J}|t-t_{j}|\leqslant\frac{h^{J}}{J!}\sup_{0\leqslant s\leqslant h}\,\lVert f^{(J)}(s)\rVert_{E}\,.] 





@A2.SS2.p2 

@A2.SS2.p2.1 This is the Banach-valued form of the Genocchi–Hermite interpolation
remainder; see [25, Section 9]. Apply the scalar argument to  [\ell\circ f] , where  [\ell\in E^{*}]  has unit norm. Taking the supremum over all such  [\ell]  concludes the proof.




@A3 

Appendix C Gaussian analysis


@A3.p1 

@A3.p1.1 This appendix collects the Gaussian analysis used throughout
the paper.


@A3.SS1 

C.1 Basic inequalities and heat smoothing


@A3.SS1.p1 

@A3.SS1.p1.1 We recall the definition of a Wiener chaos. The  [n] -th Wiener chaos is the subspace of  [L^{2}(\gamma)] 
consisting of polynomials of degree at most  [n]  that are
orthogonal to every polynomial of degree less than  [n] .
The zeroth chaos consists of constants.
For  [F]  in the  [n] -th chaos,  [\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{OU}}F=-nF] , so
 [(1-\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{OU}})^{-1}F=(n+1)^{-1}F] .


@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1 
Derivative and divergence inequalities.


@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1 

@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1.1 Fix  [d\geqslant 1]  and let  [\gamma]  be standard Gaussian measure on  [\mathbb{R}^{d}] . We also let
 [\mathcal{H}]  be a finite-dimensional11
                1
                
                
                
                
                
                
                
              We expect that the results can be extended to the infinite-dimensional setting, but this will not be needed here. Hilbert space, and let
 [V=(V_{i})_{i\in[d]}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathcal{H}^{d}]  be smooth. Its Gaussian
divergence is





 [\delta_{\gamma}V\coloneqq\sum_{i\in[d]}(x_{i}V_{i}-\partial_{i}V_{i})\,.] 



@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1.2 It is the  [L^{2}(\gamma)] -adjoint of the gradient:





 [\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}\langle DF,V\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}}=\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}\langle F,\delta_{\gamma}V\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\,.] 

(C.1)


@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1.3 Here,  [D=\nabla]  is the usual gradient.


@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p2 

@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p2.1 Componentwise Gaussian Poincaré and two applications of
(C.1) give





 [\lVert F-\mathbb{E}F\rVert_{L^{2}(\gamma)}\leqslant\lVert DF\rVert_{L^{2}(\gamma;\mathrm{HS})}\,,\qquad\lVert\delta_{\gamma}V\rVert_{L^{2}(\gamma)}^{2}\leqslant\lVert V\rVert_{L^{2}(\gamma;\mathrm{HS})}^{2}+\lVert DV\rVert_{L^{2}(\gamma;\mathrm{HS})}^{2}\,.] 

(C.2)


@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p2.2 Indeed, the exact identity behind the second inequality is





 [\lVert\delta_{\gamma}V\rVert_{L^{2}(\gamma)}^{2}=\lVert V\rVert_{L^{2}(\gamma;\mathrm{HS})}^{2}+\sum_{i,j\in[d]}\mathbb{E}\langle\partial_{j}V_{i},\partial_{i}V_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\,;] 



@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p2.3 the last sum is at most  [\lVert DV\rVert_{L^{2}(\gamma;\mathrm{HS})}^{2}]  by
Cauchy–Schwarz. If  [\gamma_{t}\coloneqq\mathsf{N}(0,tI)] , then





 [\delta_{\gamma_{t}}V\coloneqq\sum_{i\in[d]}(t^{-1}x_{i}V_{i}-\partial_{i}V_{i})\,,] 



@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p2.4 and the change of variables  [x=\sqrt{t}\,z]  gives





 [\lVert\delta_{\gamma_{t}}V\rVert_{L^{2}(\gamma_{t})}^{2}\leqslant t^{-1}\,\lVert V\rVert_{L^{2}(\gamma_{t};\mathrm{HS})}^{2}+\lVert DV\rVert_{L^{2}(\gamma_{t};\mathrm{HS})}^{2}\,.] 

(C.3)


@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p2.5 We also need  [L^{q}]  versions of these inequalities, which requires some groundwork. Let





 [\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{OU}}\coloneqq\Delta-\langle x,\nabla\rangle=-\delta_{\gamma}D\,,\qquad Q_{t}\coloneqq\exp(t\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{OU}})\,.] 

(C.4)


@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p2.6 Mehler’s
formula reads





 [(Q_{t}F)(x)=\mathbb{E}F\bigl(e^{-t}\,x+\sqrt{1-e^{-2t}}\,G\bigr)\,.] 

(C.5)


@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p2.7 Recall the basic facts





 [\lVert Q_{t}F\rVert_{L^{q}(\gamma;\mathcal{H})}\leqslant\lVert F\rVert_{L^{q}(\gamma;\mathcal{H})}\,,\qquad Q_{t}^{*}=Q_{t}\,,\qquad DQ_{t}F=e^{-t}Q_{t}DF\,.] 

(C.6)


@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p2.8 These statements remain valid for functions with values in a
finite-dimensional Hilbert space.


@A3.Thmtheorem1 
Lemma C.1 (Gaussian Riesz estimate).

@A3.Thmtheorem1.p1 

@A3.Thmtheorem1.p1.1 Let  [\mathcal{H}]  be an  [m] -dimensional Hilbert space and let
 [1<q\leqslant 2] . For every
 [F\in L^{q}(\gamma;\mathcal{H})] ,





 [\lVert D(-\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{OU}})^{-1/2}F\rVert_{L^{q}(\gamma;\mathrm{HS})}\leqslant\frac{C}{q-1}\,\lVert F\rVert_{L^{q}(\gamma;\mathcal{H})}\,.] 

(C.7)


@A3.Thmtheorem1.p1.2 The constant is independent of  [d]  and  [m] .



@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.1 
Proof.

@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p3 

@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p3.1 The estimate in [5, Theorem 4] states, for centered scalar
 [f] , that





 [\lVert D(-\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{OU}})^{-1/2}f\rVert_{L^{q}(\gamma)}\leqslant 2\,\Bigl[\bigl(q\vee\frac{q}{q-1}\bigr)-1\Bigr]\,\lVert f\rVert_{L^{q}(\gamma)}=\frac{2}{q-1}\,\lVert f\rVert_{L^{q}(\gamma)}\,,\qquad 1<q\leqslant 2\,.] 

(C.8)


@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p3.2 Since
 [(-\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{OU}})^{-1/2}]  annihilates constants, applying
(C.8) to  [f-\mathbb{E}f]  and using
 [\lVert f-\mathbb{E}f\rVert_{L^{q}}\leqslant 2\,\lVert f\rVert_{L^{q}}]  gives the same estimate for
arbitrary scalar  [f] , up to a factor two.


@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p4 

@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p4.1 We now pass to a Hilbert-valued  [F] . If  [(h_{j})_{j\in[m]}\subset\mathcal{H}] ,
and  [(g_{j})_{j\in[m]}]  are independent standard Gaussians, then for  [1<q\leqslant 2] 





 [c_{0}\,\biggl(\sum_{j\in[m]}\lVert h_{j}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}\leqslant\Bigl\{\mathbb{E}\Bigl\lVert\sum_{j\in[m]}g_{j}h_{j}\Bigr\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{q}\Bigr\}^{1/q}\leqslant\biggl(\sum_{j\in[m]}\lVert h_{j}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}] 

(C.9)


@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p4.2 for a universal  [c_{0}>0] .
Indeed, this follows from [35, Corollary 3.2].


@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p5 

@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p5.1 Expand  [F=\sum_{j\in[m]}F_{j}e_{j}]  in an orthonormal basis of
 [\mathcal{H}] . Apply the lower bound in
(C.9) pointwise to the vectors
 [D(-\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{OU}})^{-1/2}F_{j}] , then apply
(C.8) to each randomized scalar function,
and finally apply the upper bound in
(C.9) pointwise to the vectors
 [F_{j}] . Fubini’s theorem gives





 [\displaystyle\lVert D(-\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{OU}})^{-1/2}F\rVert_{L^{q}(\gamma;\mathrm{HS})}] 
 [\displaystyle\leqslant C\,\Bigl\{\mathbb{E}\Bigl\lVert D(-\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{OU}})^{-1/2}\sum_{j\in[m]}g_{j}F_{j}\Bigr\rVert_{L^{q}(\gamma)}^{q}\Bigr\}^{1/q}] 





 [\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{C}{q-1}\,\Bigl\{\mathbb{E}\Bigl\lVert\sum_{j\in[m]}g_{j}F_{j}\Bigr\rVert_{L^{q}(\gamma)}^{q}\Bigr\}^{1/q}\leqslant\frac{C}{q-1}\,\lVert F\rVert_{L^{q}(\gamma;\mathcal{H})}\,.\qed] 





@A3.Thmtheorem2 
Lemma C.2 (Second-order Gaussian Riesz estimate).

@A3.Thmtheorem2.p1 

@A3.Thmtheorem2.p1.1 Let  [\mathcal{H}]  be an  [m] -dimensional Hilbert space and let
 [1<q\leqslant 2] . Every smooth compactly supported
 [F:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathcal{H}]  satisfies





 [\lVert(1-\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{OU}})^{-1}D^{2}F\rVert_{L^{q}(\gamma;\mathrm{HS})}=\Bigl\lVert\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-t}Q_{t}D^{2}F\,\mathrm{d}t\Bigr\rVert_{L^{q}(\gamma;\mathrm{HS})}\leqslant\frac{C}{(q-1)^{2}}\,\lVert F\rVert_{L^{q}(\gamma;\mathcal{H})}\,.] 

(C.10)


@A3.Thmtheorem2.p1.2 The constant is independent of  [d]  and  [m] .
The operator on the left extends uniquely to all of
 [L^{q}(\gamma;\mathcal{H})] .



@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.2 
Proof.

@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p6 

@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p6.1 Define the resolvent





 [\mathcal{R}\coloneqq(1-\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{OU}})^{-1}=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-t}Q_{t}\,\mathrm{d}t\,.] 



@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p6.2 The contraction in (C.6) gives
 [\lVert\mathcal{R}\rVert_{L^{q}(\gamma)\to L^{q}(\gamma)}\leqslant 1] . If
 [a_{j}\coloneqq\binom{1/2}{j}] , then, for  [j\geqslant 1] ,





 [|a_{j}|=\frac{1}{2j-1}\,\binom{2j}{j}\,4^{-j}\leqslant\frac{1}{(2j-1)\sqrt{j+1}}\leqslant Cj^{-3/2}\,.] 



@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p6.3 Consequently,  [\sum_{j\geqslant 0}|a_{j}|<\infty] , and





 [\mathcal{M}\coloneqq\sum_{j\geqslant 0}a_{j}\mathcal{R}^{j}\,,] 

(C.11)


@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p6.4 converges absolutely in operator norm with
 [\lVert\mathcal{M}\rVert_{L^{q}(\gamma)\to L^{q}(\gamma)}\leqslant C] .


@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p7 

@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p7.1 On the  [n] -th Wiener
chaos,  [\mathcal{R}]  has multiplier  [(n+1)^{-1}] , so for every
 [F]  in that chaos,





 [\mathcal{M}F=\sum_{j\geqslant 0}\frac{a_{j}}{(n+1)^{j}}\,F=\Bigl(\frac{n+2}{n+1}\Bigr)^{1/2}\,F\,.] 

(C.12)



@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p8 

@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p8.1 Let  [\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{H}}\coloneqq D(-\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{OU}})^{-1/2}]  on
 [\mathcal{H}] -valued functions. A check on
each Wiener chaos gives the exact factorization





 [\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-t}Q_{t}D^{2}F\,\mathrm{d}t=\mathcal{M}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{H}}F\,.] 

(C.13)


@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p8.2 Indeed, if  [F]  belongs to the  [n] -th chaos,  [n\geqslant 2] , then the
left-hand side of (C.13) equals





 [\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-(n-1)t}D^{2}F\,\mathrm{d}t=\frac{D^{2}F}{n-1}\,.] 

(C.14)


@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p8.3 Differentiation maps the  [n] -th Wiener chaos into the
 [(n-1)] -st chaos; see [51, Proposition 1.2.2].
Consequently, for  [F]  in the  [n] -th chaos,  [n\geqslant 1] ,





 [\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{H}}F=n^{-1/2}DF] 



@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p8.4 belongs to the  [(n-1)] -st chaos.
Thus, the two Riesz transforms on the right-hand side of (C.13) produce
 [D^{2}F/\sqrt{n\,(n-1)}] , and
(C.12), applied on the
 [(n-2)] -chaos, supplies  [\sqrt{n/(n-1)}] . Their product is
exactly (C.14). This proves
(C.13) on Hermite polynomials.
The right-hand side extends to a bounded operator on  [L^{q}(\gamma;\mathcal{H})]  by
(C.7) and
(C.11). For a smooth
compactly supported  [F] , its Hermite expansion converges in the
domain of  [D^{2}]  in  [L^{2}] , so both sides of
(C.13) are the same  [L^{2}] 
limit. Thus the integral on the left is precisely this bounded
extension in the setting used below.


@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p9 

@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p9.1 Apply (C.7) first with target
 [\mathcal{H}] , then with target
 [\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{d}] , and use
the operator norm bound following
(C.11). The factorization
(C.13) yields





 [\displaystyle\Bigl\lVert\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-t}Q_{t}D^{2}F\,\mathrm{d}t\Bigr\rVert_{L^{q}(\gamma;\mathrm{HS})}=\lVert\mathcal{M}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{H}}F\rVert_{L^{q}(\gamma;\mathrm{HS})}] 
 [\displaystyle\leqslant C\,\Bigl(\frac{1}{q-1}\Bigr)^{2}\,\lVert F\rVert_{L^{q}(\gamma;\mathcal{H})}\,.\qed] 





@A3.Thmtheorem3 
Lemma C.3 (Gaussian Sobolev inequalities).

@A3.Thmtheorem3.p1 

@A3.Thmtheorem3.p1.1 Let  [q\geqslant 2] . If
 [F\in W^{1,q}(\gamma;\mathcal{H})] , then





 [\lVert F-\mathbb{E}F\rVert_{L^{q}(\gamma;\mathcal{H})}\leqslant C\sqrt{q}\,\lVert DF\rVert_{L^{q}(\gamma;\mathrm{HS})}\,.] 



@A3.Thmtheorem3.p1.2 If  [V\in W^{1,q}(\gamma;\mathcal{H}^{d})]  and
 [\delta_{\gamma}V]  is its row divergence, then





 [\lVert\delta_{\gamma}V\rVert_{L^{q}(\gamma;\mathcal{H})}\leqslant C\sqrt{q}\,\lVert\mathbb{E}V\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}+Cq^{2}\,\lVert DV\rVert_{L^{q}(\gamma;\mathrm{HS})}\,.] 

(C.15)


@A3.Thmtheorem3.p1.3 If we replace  [\gamma]  by  [\gamma_{t}] , the centered Gaussian measure of covariance  [tI] , then the first term on
the right side of (C.15) is multiplied by
 [t^{-1/2}] .



@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.3 
Proof.

@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p10 

@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p10.1 The first estimate follows from Pisier’s Gaussian convexity inequality [54, Theorem 2.2], applied with the convex
function  [u\mapsto\lVert u\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{q}] .


@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p11 

@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p11.1 As we were unable to find a proof of the divergence estimate with explicit dependence on  [q]  in the literature, we give a proof here using
Lemma C.2.
First suppose that  [V]  is smooth and compactly supported, and that
 [\mathbb{E}V=0] . Strong continuity and ergodicity of the Mehler semigroup
(C.5) give





 [\int_{\varepsilon}^{T}(-\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{OU}})Q_{t}V\,\mathrm{d}t=Q_{\varepsilon}V-Q_{T}V\longrightarrow V\quad\text{in }L^{q}(\gamma)] 

(C.16)


@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p11.2 as  [\varepsilon\searrow 0]  and  [T\nearrow\infty] . For a smooth
compactly supported test function  [F:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathcal{H}] , the adjoint
identity (C.1), the identity
 [-\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{OU}}=\delta_{\gamma}D]  in
(C.4), and
(C.16) give





 [\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\langle F,\delta_{\gamma}V\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}] 
 [\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\langle DF,V\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\langle DF,\delta_{\gamma}DQ_{t}V\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}}\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\langle D^{2}F,DQ_{t}V\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}}\,\mathrm{d}t] 





 [\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigl\langle DV,\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-t}Q_{t}D^{2}F\,\mathrm{d}t\Bigr\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}}\,.] 

(C.17)


@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p11.3 The last line uses both the
commutation and self-adjointness statements in
(C.6). All integrals can first be taken over
 [[\varepsilon,T]] ; the limit follows from
(C.16) and from the integrable
factor  [e^{-t}] .


@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p12 

@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p12.1 Set  [q^{*}\coloneqq q/(q-1)] , so that
 [(q^{*}-1)^{-1}=q-1] . Apply
(C.10) at exponent  [q^{*}]  and use
Hölder’s inequality in
(C.17) to obtain





 [\bigl|\mathbb{E}\langle F,\delta_{\gamma}V\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\bigr|\leqslant C\,(q-1)^{2}\,\lVert DV\rVert_{L^{q}(\gamma;\mathrm{HS})}\,\lVert F\rVert_{L^{q^{*}}(\gamma;\mathcal{H})}\,.] 



@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p12.2 Duality between  [L^{q^{*}}(\gamma;\mathcal{H})]  and
 [L^{q}(\gamma;\mathcal{H})]  therefore yields





 [\lVert\delta_{\gamma}V\rVert_{L^{q}(\gamma;\mathcal{H})}\leqslant C\,(q-1)^{2}\,\lVert DV\rVert_{L^{q}(\gamma;\mathrm{HS})}\qquad\text{if }\mathbb{E}V=0\,.] 

(C.18)



@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p13 

@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p13.1 For a general  [V] , apply
(C.18) to  [V-\mathbb{E}V] . The
remaining term is
 [\delta_{\gamma}(\mathbb{E}V)(x)=\sum_{j\in[d]}x_{j}\,(\mathbb{E}V)_{j}] . The map
 [x\mapsto\delta_{\gamma}(\mathbb{E}V)(x)]  is a centered
 [\mathcal{H}] -valued Gaussian random vector whose  [L^{2}(\gamma)]  norm
is  [\lVert\mathbb{E}V\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}] . The standard equivalence of Gaussian
moments [35, Corollary 3.2] therefore gives





 [\lVert\delta_{\gamma}(\mathbb{E}V)\rVert_{L^{q}(\gamma;\mathcal{H})}\leqslant C\sqrt{q}\,\lVert\mathbb{E}V\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}\,.] 

(C.19)


@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p13.2 Combining (C.18) and
(C.19) proves
(C.15).


@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p14 

@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p14.1 The calculation above applies first to smooth fields. The derivative is
closable and smooth cylindrical fields are dense in the Gaussian
Sobolev norm by [51, Proposition 1.2.1], so a standard approximation argument extends the proof to vector fields in  [W^{1,q}(\gamma;\mathcal{H}^{d})] .
Finally, the last sentence follows from rescaling.
∎




@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px2 
Dual Poincaré inequality.


@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1 

@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1.1 For a centered, finite-dimensional Hilbert-valued  [F\in L^{2}(\gamma;\mathcal{H})] , define





 [\lVert F\rVert_{\dot{H}^{-1}(\gamma;\mathcal{H})}\coloneqq\sup\bigl\{\mathbb{E}\langle F,H\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}:\mathbb{E}H=0,\quad\lVert DH\rVert_{L^{2}(\gamma;\mathrm{HS})}\leqslant 1\bigr\}\,.] 



@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1.2 We then have





 [\lVert F\rVert_{\dot{H}^{-1}(\gamma;\mathcal{H})}=\lVert D(-\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{OU}})^{-1}F\rVert_{L^{2}(\gamma;\mathrm{HS})}\,.] 



@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1.3 The following is the dual form of the Gaussian Poincaré inequality, see [33].


@A3.Thmtheorem4 
Lemma C.4 (Dual Gaussian Poincaré inequality).

@A3.Thmtheorem4.p1 

@A3.Thmtheorem4.p1.1 For all such  [F] ,





 [\lVert F\rVert_{\dot{H}^{-1}(\gamma;\mathcal{H})}\leqslant\lVert F\rVert_{L^{2}(\gamma;\mathcal{H})}\,.] 






@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px3 
Heat equation estimates.


@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px3.p1 

@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px3.p1.1 For later reference, let  [(P_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}]  denote the Euclidean heat
semigroup,





 [(P_{t}F)(x)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}F(x+\sqrt{t}\,G)\,.] 



@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px3.p1.2 Thus  [\partial_{t}P_{t}F=\frac{1}{2}\Delta P_{t}F] . If  [F]  is weakly
differentiable, differentiation commutes with the heat semigroup, whereas
Gaussian integration by parts gives the derivative formula





 [DP_{t}F(x)=P_{t}(DF)(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}\,\mathbb{E}[F(x+\sqrt{t}\,G)\otimes G]\,.] 

(C.20)


@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px3.p1.3 In particular, Jensen and Bessel’s inequality give





 [\displaystyle\lVert DP_{t}F(x)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}] 
 [\displaystyle\leqslant\operatorname{Lip}(F)\,,] 




 [\displaystyle\lVert DP_{t}F(x)\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^{2}] 
 [\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{t}\inf_{a\in\mathcal{H}}\mathbb{E}\lVert F(x+\sqrt{t}\,G)-a\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\,.] 

(C.21)


@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px3.p1.4 To justify the second estimate, fix  [a\in\mathcal{H}]  and put





 [Y\coloneqq F(x+\sqrt{t}\,G)-a\,.] 



@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px3.p1.5 The orthogonal projection of  [Y\in L^{2}(\gamma;\mathcal{H})]  onto the
 [\mathcal{H}] -valued first Gaussian chaos is





 [\mathcal{P}_{1}Y\coloneqq\sum_{i\in[d]}G_{i}\,\mathbb{E}[G_{i}Y]\,.] 



@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px3.p1.6 Since the coordinate functions  [(G_{i})_{i\in[d]}]  are orthonormal in
 [L^{2}(\gamma)] , Bessel’s inequality gives





 [\sum_{i\in[d]}\lVert\mathbb{E}[G_{i}Y]\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=\mathbb{E}\lVert\mathcal{P}_{1}Y\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leqslant\mathbb{E}\lVert Y\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\,.] 



@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px3.p1.7 Moreover,  [\mathbb{E}[a\otimes G]=0] , so
(C.20) and the preceding display imply





 [t\,\lVert DP_{t}F(x)\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^{2}=\sum_{i\in[d]}\lVert\mathbb{E}[G_{i}Y]\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leqslant\mathbb{E}\lVert F(x+\sqrt{t}\,G)-a\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\,.] 



@A3.SS1.SSS0.Px3.p1.8 Taking the infimum over  [a\in\mathcal{H}]  proves
(C.21).




@A3.SS2 

C.2 Cumulants and derivatives of smoothed potentials


@A3.SS2.p1 

@A3.SS2.p1.1 For random variables  [F_{1},\ldots,F_{k}]  whose joint moment-generating
function is finite near the origin, define their joint cumulant by





 [\operatorname{Cum}(F_{1},\ldots,F_{k})\coloneqq\biggl.\partial_{t_{1}}\cdots\partial_{t_{k}}\log\mathbb{E}\exp\Bigl\{\sum_{j=1}^{k}t_{j}F_{j}\Bigr\}\biggr|_{t_{1}=\cdots=t_{k}=0}\,.] 



@A3.SS2.p1.2 If  [X]  is vector-valued,  [\operatorname{Cum}_{k}(X)]  denotes the
symmetric  [k] -tensor obtained by applying this definition to linear
functionals of  [X] .


@A3.SS2.p2 

@A3.SS2.p2.1 We record the connection with derivatives of the smoothed potential. Let
 [X_{y}\sim R_{\eta,y}] , and set





 [Z_{\eta}(y)\coloneqq\int\exp\Bigl\{-V(x)-\frac{\lVert x\rVert^{2}}{2\eta}+\frac{\langle y,x\rangle}{\eta}\Bigr\}\,\mathrm{d}x\,.] 



@A3.SS2.p2.2 Up to an additive constant,





 [V_{\eta}(y)=\frac{\lVert y\rVert^{2}}{2\eta}-\log Z_{\eta}(y)\,.] 



@A3.SS2.p2.3 Since  [\log Z_{\eta}]  is the cumulant-generating function of the exponential
family  [R_{\eta,y}] , differentiation gives, for every  [k\geqslant 2] ,





 [D^{k}V_{\eta}(y)=\mathbf{1}_{\{k=2\}}\,\eta^{-1}I-\eta^{-k}\operatorname{Cum}_{k}(X_{y})\,.] 

(C.22)



@A3.SS2.p3 

@A3.SS2.p3.1 We need the following recursive representation of cumulants.


@A3.Thmtheorem5 
Lemma C.5 (Iterated Gaussian covariance).

@A3.Thmtheorem5.p1 

@A3.Thmtheorem5.p1.1 Let  [F_{1},\ldots,F_{k}]  be smooth scalar functions of a standard Gaussian,
with bounded derivatives of every positive order. Define





 [\displaystyle\Gamma_{1}(F_{1})] 
 [\displaystyle\coloneqq F_{1}-\mathbb{E}F_{1}\,,] 




 [\displaystyle\Gamma_{j}(F_{1},\ldots,F_{j})] 
 [\displaystyle\coloneqq\langle DF_{j},D(-\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{OU}})^{-1}\Gamma_{j-1}(F_{1},\ldots,F_{j-1})\rangle\,,\quad j\geqslant 2\,.] 

(C.23)


@A3.Thmtheorem5.p1.2 Then





 [\operatorname{Cum}(F_{1},\ldots,F_{k})=\sum_{\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{k-1}}\mathbb{E}\Gamma_{k}(F_{1},F_{\pi(2)},\ldots,F_{\pi(k)})\,,] 



@A3.Thmtheorem5.p1.3 where each permutation is over  [\{2,\dotsc,k\}] .



@A3.SS2.2 
Proof.

@A3.SS2.p4 

@A3.SS2.p4.1 This is a symmetrized form of [50, Theorem 4.4].
∎



@A3.Thmtheorem6 
Lemma C.6 (Cumulants of a near-identity Gaussian image).

@A3.Thmtheorem6.p1 

@A3.Thmtheorem6.p1.1 Suppose  [T:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}]  is locally Lipschitz and, almost everywhere,





 [\lVert DT\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant 1\,,\qquad\lVert DT-I\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant\varepsilon_{T}\,,\qquad 0\leqslant\varepsilon_{T}\leqslant 1\,.] 



@A3.Thmtheorem6.p1.2 If  [G]  is standard Gaussian, then, for every  [k\geqslant 3] ,





 [\displaystyle\sup_{\lVert v\rVert=1}\,\lVert\operatorname{Cum}_{k}(T(G))[v,\cdot^{k-1}]\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}] 
 [\displaystyle\leqslant 2\,(k-1)!\,\varepsilon_{T}\,,] 




 [\displaystyle\lVert\operatorname{Cum}_{k}(T(G))\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}] 
 [\displaystyle\leqslant 2\,(k-1)!\,\varepsilon_{T}\sqrt{d}\,.] 

(C.24)


@A3.Thmtheorem6.p1.3 Here,  [A[v,\cdot^{k-1}]]  denotes the  [(k-1)] -tensor obtained by fixing
the first argument of a  [k] -tensor  [A]  to  [v] , leaving the remaining
 [k-1]  arguments free. In particular,





 [\lVert A[v,\cdot^{k-1}]\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^{2}=\sum_{j_{2},\ldots,j_{k}\in[d]}\bigl|A[v,e_{j_{2}},\ldots,e_{j_{k}}]\bigr|^{2}\,,] 



@A3.Thmtheorem6.p1.4 where  [(e_{j})_{j\in[d]}]  is any orthonormal basis of  [\mathbb{R}^{d}] .



@A3.SS2.3 
Proof.

@A3.SS2.p5 

@A3.SS2.p5.1 First take  [T]  smooth.
For fixed  [v] , define the  [(j-1)] -tensor
 [\Gamma_{j}(v)]  by





 [[\Gamma_{j}(v)]_{i_{2},\ldots,i_{j}}\coloneqq\Gamma_{j}\bigl(\langle v,T(G)\rangle,T_{i_{2}}(G),\ldots,T_{i_{j}}(G)\bigr)\,,\qquad i_{2},\ldots,i_{j}\in[d]\,,] 



@A3.SS2.p5.2 using the scalar recursion (C.23).
For  [j=2] , its  [i] -th entry is
 [\langle DT_{i}(G),D(-\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{OU}})^{-1}(\langle v,T(G)\rangle)\rangle] ,
so collecting the entries gives
 [\Gamma_{2}(v)=DT(G)\,D(-\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{OU}})^{-1}(\langle v,T(G)\rangle)] .
Moreover,  [\langle v,G\rangle]  belongs to the first Wiener chaos, so
 [D(-\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{OU}})^{-1}(\langle v,G\rangle)=v] .
Subtracting this linear contribution yields





 [\displaystyle\lVert\Gamma_{2}(v)-v\rVert_{L^{2}(\gamma)}] 
 [\displaystyle\leqslant\lVert(DT-I)\,v\rVert_{L^{2}(\gamma)}+\bigl\lVert DT\,\bigl(D(-\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{OU}})^{-1}(\langle v,T\rangle)-v\bigr)\bigr\rVert_{L^{2}(\gamma)}] 





 [\displaystyle\leqslant\varepsilon_{T}\,\lVert v\rVert+\lVert DT\,D(-\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{OU}})^{-1}(\langle v,T-I\rangle)\rVert_{L^{2}(\gamma)}] 





 [\displaystyle\leqslant\varepsilon_{T}\,\lVert v\rVert+\lVert D(-\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{OU}})^{-1}(\langle v,T-I\rangle)\rVert_{L^{2}(\gamma)}] 





 [\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{T}\,\lVert v\rVert+\lVert\langle v,T-I\rangle\rVert_{\dot{H}^{-1}(\gamma)}\leqslant\varepsilon_{T}\,\lVert v\rVert+\lVert D\langle v,T-I\rangle\rVert_{L^{2}(\gamma)}\leqslant 2\varepsilon_{T}\,\lVert v\rVert\,,] 



@A3.SS2.p5.3 where we applied Lemma C.4 and the Gaussian Poincaré inequality.
This implies





 [\lVert\Gamma_{2}(v)-\mathbb{E}\Gamma_{2}(v)\rVert_{L^{2}(\gamma;\mathrm{HS})}\leqslant 2\varepsilon_{T}\,\lVert v\rVert\,.] 

(C.25)


@A3.SS2.p5.4 For  [j\geqslant 2] , collect the free indices into a tensor. Then,  [\Gamma_{j+1}]  is a
contraction of  [DT]  with
 [D(-\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{OU}})^{-1}\Gamma_{j}] ; hence, by  [\lVert DT\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant 1]  and Lemma C.4,





 [\lVert\Gamma_{j+1}(v)\rVert_{L^{2}(\gamma;\mathrm{HS})}\leqslant\lVert D(-\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{OU}})^{-1}\Gamma_{j}(v)\rVert_{L^{2}(\gamma;\mathrm{HS})}=\lVert\Gamma_{j}(v)\rVert_{\dot{H}^{-1}(\gamma;\mathrm{HS})}\leqslant\lVert\Gamma_{j}(v)-\mathbb{E}\Gamma_{j}(v)\rVert_{L^{2}(\gamma;\mathrm{HS})}\,.] 



@A3.SS2.p5.5 Starting from (C.25) thus gives
 [\lVert\Gamma_{k}(v)\rVert_{L^{2}(\gamma;\mathrm{HS})}\leqslant 2\varepsilon_{T}\,\lVert v\rVert]  for every ordering.
Thus Lemma C.5, Minkowski, and Jensen imply





 [\lVert\operatorname{Cum}_{k}(T(G))[v,\cdot^{k-1}]\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}\leqslant\sum_{\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{k-1}}\lVert\mathbb{E}\Gamma_{k}^{\pi}(v)\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}\leqslant 2\,(k-1)!\,\varepsilon_{T}\,\lVert v\rVert\,.] 



@A3.SS2.p5.6 Squaring and summing over an orthonormal basis proves
(C.24). A standard approximation argument extends the bound to locally Lipschitz  [T] .
∎




@A3.SS3 

C.3 Proofs of the second-order Wasserstein estimates


@A3.SS3.2 
Proof of Lemma 4.11.

@A3.SS3.p1 

@A3.SS3.p1.1 By approximation, it is enough to treat smooth  [f] . Throughout the
proof, for functions of  [(z,w)] , super- or sub-scripts  [1]  and  [2]  indicate operations in the first and second
arguments, respectively. Thus,  [P_{s}^{(2)}]  is the heat semigroup in the
second argument,  [D_{1}]  and  [D_{2}]  are the corresponding derivatives,
 [\delta_{1}]  is Gaussian row divergence in the first argument under
 [\mathsf{N}(0,I_{m})] , and  [\delta_{2,t}]  is Gaussian row divergence in the
second argument under  [\mathsf{N}(0,tI_{n})] .


@A3.SS3.p2 

@A3.SS3.p2.1 Let
 [(W_{t})_{0\leqslant t\leqslant 1}]  be Brownian motion in  [\mathbb{R}^{n}] , independent of
 [Z] , and define





 [u_{t}(z,w)\coloneqq P_{1-t}^{(2)}f(z,\cdot)(w)\,,\qquad X_{t}\coloneqq Z+u_{t}(Z,W_{t})\,.] 



@A3.SS3.p2.2 Thus,  [X_{0}=Z+\bar{f}(Z)] , while  [X_{1}]  has the law of  [Z+f(Z,G)] .
Writing  [(\mathcal{F}_{t}^{W})_{0\leqslant t\leqslant 1}]  for the Brownian filtration, the Markov property
gives





 [u_{t}(z,W_{t})=\mathbb{E}[f(z,W_{1})\mid\mathcal{F}_{t}^{W}]\,,\qquad(0\leqslant t\leqslant 1)\,.] 



@A3.SS3.p2.3 Consequently,  [(X_{t})_{0\leqslant t\leqslant 1}]  is a martingale with respect to the enlarged
filtration  [(\sigma(Z)\vee\mathcal{F}_{t}^{W})_{0\leqslant t\leqslant 1}] . Since  [u] 
solves the backward heat equation with respect to  [w] ,
Itô’s formula identifies its martingale representation as





 [\mathrm{d}X_{t}=D_{2}u_{t}(Z,W_{t})\,\mathrm{d}W_{t}\,.] 



@A3.SS3.p2.4 Define





 [\Sigma_{t}(z,w)\coloneqq D_{2}u_{t}(z,w)D_{2}u_{t}(z,w)^{\mathsf{T}}\,.] 



@A3.SS3.p2.5 A second application of Itô’s formula gives, for any smooth scalar test
function  [\varphi] ,





 [\partial_{t}\,\mathbb{E}\varphi(X_{t})=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\langle\Sigma_{t}(Z,W_{t}),D^{2}\varphi(X_{t})\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}}\,.] 



@A3.SS3.p2.6 To estimate the Wasserstein distance, we recast this as a continuity equation. The chain rule gives





 [\displaystyle D_{1}\nabla\varphi(z+u_{t}(z,w))] 
 [\displaystyle=D^{2}\varphi(z+u_{t}(z,w))\,(I+D_{1}u_{t}(z,w))\,,] 




 [\displaystyle D_{2}\nabla\varphi(z+u_{t}(z,w))] 
 [\displaystyle=D^{2}\varphi(z+u_{t}(z,w))\,D_{2}u_{t}(z,w)\,.] 



@A3.SS3.p2.7 Since  [\Sigma_{t}]  and  [D^{2}\varphi]  are symmetric, it follows that






 [\displaystyle\langle\Sigma_{t}(z,w),D^{2}\varphi(z+u_{t}(z,w))\rangle] 





 [\displaystyle\qquad={}\langle\Sigma_{t}(z,w),D_{1}\nabla\varphi(z+u_{t}(z,w))\rangle-\langle D_{1}u_{t}(z,w)\,D_{2}u_{t}(z,w),D_{2}\nabla\varphi(z+u_{t}(z,w))\rangle\,.] 



@A3.SS3.p2.8 Here and in the sequel, all derivatives of  [u_{t}]  and  [\Sigma_{t}] 
are understood as evaluated at  [(Z,W_{t})] .
Applying the divergence adjoint identity
(C.1) in each Gaussian variable gives





 [\mathbb{E}\langle\Sigma_{t},D^{2}\varphi(X_{t})\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}}=\mathbb{E}\langle\delta_{1}\Sigma_{t}-\delta_{2,t}(D_{1}u_{t}D_{2}u_{t}),\nabla\varphi(X_{t})\rangle\,.] 



@A3.SS3.p2.9 It follows that





 [\partial_{t}\mathbb{E}\varphi(X_{t})=\mathbb{E}\langle V_{t},\nabla\varphi(X_{t})\rangle\,,\qquad V_{t}\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\,\{\delta_{1}\Sigma_{t}-\delta_{2,t}(D_{1}u_{t}D_{2}u_{t})\}\,.] 



@A3.SS3.p2.10 Consequently, the evolution of  [\law(LX_{t})]  satisfies the continuity equation





 [\partial_{t}\law(LX_{t})+\operatorname{div}\bigl(v_{t}\law(LX_{t})\bigr)=0\,,] 



@A3.SS3.p2.11 where the velocity field is given by





 [v_{t}(y)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[LV_{t}\mid LX_{t}=y]\,,\qquad\lVert v_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}\leqslant\lVert LV_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}\,.] 




@A3.SS3.p3 

@A3.SS3.p3.1 It remains to estimate this explicit velocity. The heat semigroup preserves
the first derivative bounds, while (C.21) applied
to the weak derivatives of  [f]  gives the following bounds:





 [\displaystyle\lVert D_{1}u_{t}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}] 
 [\displaystyle\leqslant A\,,] 
 [\displaystyle\lVert D_{2}u_{t}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}] 
 [\displaystyle\leqslant B\,,] 




 [\displaystyle\lVert D_{1}D_{2}u_{t}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}\vee\lVert D_{2}D_{1}u_{t}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}] 
 [\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{A\sqrt{m}}{\sqrt{1-t}}\,,] 
 [\displaystyle\lVert D_{2}^{2}u_{t}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}] 
 [\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{B\sqrt{m}}{\sqrt{1-t}}\,.] 



@A3.SS3.p3.2 Here, we used
 [\lVert D_{1}f\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}\leqslant\sqrt{m}\,A]  and
 [\lVert D_{2}f\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}\leqslant\sqrt{m}\,B] . The product rule and
 [\lVert RS\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}\leqslant\lVert R\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\,\lVert S\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}]  therefore give





 [\displaystyle\lVert\Sigma_{t}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}] 
 [\displaystyle\leqslant B^{2}\sqrt{m}\,,] 
 [\displaystyle\lVert D_{1}\Sigma_{t}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}] 
 [\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{2AB\sqrt{m}}{\sqrt{1-t}}\,,] 




 [\displaystyle\lVert D_{1}u_{t}D_{2}u_{t}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}] 
 [\displaystyle\leqslant AB\sqrt{m}\,,] 
 [\displaystyle\lVert D_{2}(D_{1}u_{t}D_{2}u_{t})\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}] 
 [\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{2AB\sqrt{m}}{\sqrt{1-t}}\,.] 



@A3.SS3.p3.3 Every column of  [D_{2}u_{t}]  lies in  [E] , so
 [\operatorname{range}(\Sigma_{t})\subseteq E] . Since  [E]  is a fixed linear subspace,
differentiating the columns of  [\Sigma_{t}]  in  [z]  preserves membership
in  [E] , and hence  [\delta_{1}\Sigma_{t}\in E] .


@A3.SS3.p4 

@A3.SS3.p4.1 We now apply the Gaussian divergence bounds conditionally in each
variable. First,
(C.2) in the  [z] -variable gives





 [\displaystyle\lVert L\delta_{1}\Sigma_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}] 
 [\displaystyle=\lVert\delta_{1}(L\Sigma_{t})\rVert_{L^{2}}\leqslant\lVert L\Sigma_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathrm{HS})}+\lVert LD_{1}\Sigma_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathrm{HS})}] 





 [\displaystyle\leqslant\sqrt{m}\,\Bigl\{\lVert L\Pi_{E}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\,B^{2}+2\,\lVert L\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\,\frac{AB}{\sqrt{1-t}}\Bigr\}\,.] 



@A3.SS3.p4.2 Here we used  [L\Sigma_{t}=L\Pi_{E}\Sigma_{t}]  in the first term. Next,
the estimate
(C.3) in the  [w] -variable gives





 [\displaystyle\lVert L\delta_{2,t}(D_{1}u_{t}D_{2}u_{t})\rVert_{L^{2}}] 
 [\displaystyle={}\lVert\delta_{2,t}(LD_{1}u_{t}D_{2}u_{t})\rVert_{L^{2}}] 





 [\displaystyle\leqslant t^{-1/2}\,\lVert LD_{1}u_{t}D_{2}u_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathrm{HS})}+\lVert LD_{2}(D_{1}u_{t}D_{2}u_{t})\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathrm{HS})}] 





 [\displaystyle\leqslant\sqrt{m}\,\lVert L\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\,AB\,\{t^{-1/2}+2\,(1-t)^{-1/2}\}\,.] 



@A3.SS3.p4.3 Combining these two estimates in the definition of  [V_{t}]  yields





 [\lVert LV_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}\leqslant\sqrt{m}\,\Bigl\{\frac{1}{2}\,\lVert L\Pi_{E}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\,B^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\,\lVert L\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\,AB\,t^{-1/2}+2\,\lVert L\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\,AB\,(1-t)^{-1/2}\Bigr\}\,.] 



@A3.SS3.p4.4 The first singularity comes from rescaling Gaussian divergence under
 [\mathsf{N}(0,tI_{n})] , and the second from differentiating the heat extension.


@A3.SS3.p5 

@A3.SS3.p5.1 Since  [\int_{0}^{1}t^{-1/2}\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)^{-1/2}\,\mathrm{d}t=2] , we have





 [\displaystyle W_{2}(\law(LX_{\varepsilon}),\law(LX_{1-\varepsilon}))] 
 [\displaystyle\leqslant\int_{\varepsilon}^{1-\varepsilon}\lVert v_{t}(LX_{t})\rVert_{L^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}t\leqslant\sqrt{m}\,\Bigl\{\frac{1}{2}\,\lVert L\Pi_{E}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\,B^{2}+5\,\lVert L\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\,AB\Bigr\}\,.] 



@A3.SS3.p5.2 We can then let  [\varepsilon\searrow 0] . This proves
(4.21).
∎



@A3.SS3.3 
Proof of Lemma 5.5.

@A3.SS3.p6 

@A3.SS3.p6.1 Use the notation and repeat the proof of
Lemma 4.11 through the construction of
 [u_{t},X_{t},\Sigma_{t},V_{t}] , and  [v_{t}] , including all the displayed
pointwise derivative estimates. Conditional expectation is now used as
an  [L^{q}]  contraction. Applying
(C.15) conditionally in the  [z] -variable,
and retaining separately its mean and derivative terms, gives





 [\lVert L\delta_{1}\Sigma_{t}\rVert_{L^{q}}\leqslant C\sqrt{m}\,\bigl\{\sqrt{q}\,\lVert L\Pi_{E}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\,B^{2}+q^{2}\,\lVert L\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\,AB\,(1-t)^{-1/2}\bigr\}\,.] 



@A3.SS3.p6.2 Its rescaled version in the  [w] -variable similarly gives





 [\lVert L\delta_{2,t}(D_{1}u_{t}D_{2}u_{t})\rVert_{L^{q}}\leqslant C\sqrt{m}\,\lVert L\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\,AB\,\bigl\{\sqrt{q}\,t^{-1/2}+q^{2}\,(1-t)^{-1/2}\bigr\}\,.] 



@A3.SS3.p6.3 Integrating these bounds proves
(5.4).
∎




@A3.SS4 

C.4 Gaussian polynomial bounds


@A3.SS4.p1 

@A3.SS4.p1.1 For this subsection,  [D]  denotes the gradient in  [p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}] , and the
Gaussian divergence is the operator defined above with  [x]  replaced by
 [p] .


@A3.Thmtheorem7 
Lemma C.7 (Gaussian polynomial estimates).

@A3.Thmtheorem7.p1 

@A3.Thmtheorem7.p1.1 Let  [r\geqslant 0]  be an integer. If  [F:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathcal{H}]  is a polynomial of total
degree at most  [r] , then





 [\lVert DF\rVert_{L^{2}(\gamma;\mathrm{HS})}\leqslant\sqrt{r}\,\lVert F\rVert_{L^{2}(\gamma)}\,.] 



@A3.Thmtheorem7.p1.2 If  [V:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathcal{H}^{d}]  is a polynomial vector field of total degree
at most  [r] , then





 [\lVert\delta_{\gamma}V\rVert_{L^{2}(\gamma)}\leqslant\sqrt{r+1}\,\lVert V\rVert_{L^{2}(\gamma;\mathrm{HS})}\,.] 





@A3.SS4.p2 

@A3.SS4.p2.1 These estimates are standard; see [51, Propositions 1.2.2 and 1.3.1], restricting to Wiener chaoses of degree at
most  [r] .
We also need  [L^{q}]  versions of these inequalities. We remark that the constants below are likely not sharp.


@A3.Thmtheorem8 
Lemma C.8 ( [L^{q}]  Gaussian polynomial estimates).

@A3.Thmtheorem8.p1 

@A3.Thmtheorem8.p1.1 Let  [q\geqslant 2] , and let  [r\geqslant 0]  be an integer. If
 [F:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathcal{H}]  is a polynomial of total degree at most  [r] , then





 [\lVert DF\rVert_{L^{q}(\gamma;\mathrm{HS})}\leqslant Cr\,\lVert F\rVert_{L^{q}(\gamma)}\,.] 

(C.26)


@A3.Thmtheorem8.p1.2 If  [V:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathcal{H}^{d}]  is a polynomial vector field of total degree
at most  [r] , then





 [\lVert\delta_{\gamma}V\rVert_{L^{q}(\gamma;\mathcal{H})}\leqslant C\,(\sqrt{q}+q^{2}r)\,\lVert V\rVert_{L^{q}(\gamma;\mathrm{HS})}\,.] 





@A3.SS4.2 
Proof.

@A3.SS4.p3 

@A3.SS4.p3.1 In this proof, we allow  [\mathcal{H}]  to be infinite-dimensional.
Write





 [F(p)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}\\
|\alpha|\leqslant r\end{subarray}}a_{\alpha}p^{\alpha}\,,\qquad a_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{H}\,,\qquad\mathcal{H}_{F}\coloneqq\operatorname{span}\{a_{\alpha}:\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d},\ |\alpha|\leqslant r\}\,.] 



@A3.SS4.p3.2 The space  [\mathcal{H}_{F}]  is finite-dimensional, and both  [F]  and  [DF]  take
values in  [\mathcal{H}_{F}] . Let
 [G_{F}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,I_{\mathcal{H}_{F}})] , independently of  [p] , and denote its
law by  [\gamma_{F}] . Viewed as an  [\mathcal{H}] -valued Gaussian,
 [G_{F}]  has covariance  [\Pi_{\mathcal{H}_{F}}] , which is finite rank and hence
trace class.


@A3.SS4.p4 

@A3.SS4.p4.1 For every fixed  [p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}] ,





 [\mathbb{E}\lVert D\langle G_{F},F\rangle(p)\rVert^{2}=\sum_{j\in[d]}\mathbb{E}\bigl|\langle G_{F},\partial_{j}F(p)\rangle\bigr|^{2}=\sum_{j\in[d]}\lVert\partial_{j}F(p)\rVert^{2}=\lVert DF(p)\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^{2}\,.] 



@A3.SS4.p4.2 Since  [q\geqslant 2] , it follows that





 [\lVert DF\rVert_{L^{q}(\gamma;\mathrm{HS})}\leqslant\lVert D\langle G_{F},F\rangle\rVert_{L^{q}(\gamma\otimes\gamma_{F})}.] 



@A3.SS4.p4.3 Moreover, the Gaussian moment bound gives, pointwise in  [p] ,





 [\bigl(\mathbb{E}\bigl|\langle G_{F},F(p)\rangle\bigr|^{q}\bigr)^{1/q}\leqslant C\sqrt{q}\,\lVert F(p)\rVert\,.] 



@A3.SS4.p4.4 The dimension-free Gaussian Bernstein–Markov inequality of
[28, Proposition 5], applied conditionally on  [G_{F}]  to the
scalar polynomial  [p\mapsto\langle G_{F},F(p)\rangle] , therefore yields





 [\displaystyle\lVert DF\rVert_{L^{q}(\gamma;\mathrm{HS})}] 
 [\displaystyle\leqslant\lVert D\langle G_{F},F\rangle\rVert_{L^{q}(\gamma\otimes\gamma_{F})}\leqslant\frac{Cr}{\sqrt{q+1}}\,\lVert\langle G_{F},F\rangle\rVert_{L^{q}(\gamma\otimes\gamma_{F})}\leqslant Cr\,\lVert F\rVert_{L^{q}(\gamma)}\,.] 



@A3.SS4.p4.5 This proves (C.26) for an arbitrary
Hilbert space  [\mathcal{H}] .


@A3.SS4.p5 

@A3.SS4.p5.1 For the divergence estimate, write





 [V_{j}(p)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}\\
|\alpha|\leqslant r\end{subarray}}a_{\alpha,j}p^{\alpha}\,,\qquad j\in[d]\,,\qquad\mathcal{H}_{V}\coloneqq\operatorname{span}\{a_{\alpha,j}:\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d},\ |\alpha|\leqslant r,\ j\in[d]\}\,.] 



@A3.SS4.p5.2 All quantities involving  [V]  take values in the finite-dimensional
spaces generated by  [\mathcal{H}_{V}] . Hence
Lemma C.3 applies with target  [\mathcal{H}_{V}] .
Using Jensen’s inequality and
(C.26) with target  [\mathcal{H}_{V}^{d}] , we
obtain





 [\displaystyle\lVert\delta_{\gamma}V\rVert_{L^{q}(\gamma;\mathcal{H})}] 
 [\displaystyle\leqslant C\sqrt{q}\,\lVert\mathbb{E}V\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}+Cq^{2}\,\lVert DV\rVert_{L^{q}(\gamma;\mathrm{HS})}\leqslant C\,(\sqrt{q}+q^{2}r)\,\lVert V\rVert_{L^{q}(\gamma;\mathrm{HS})}\qed] 







@A4 

Appendix D Replacing proximal queries by gradient queries


@A4.SS1 

D.1 Approximate proximal queries


@A4.SS1.p1 

@A4.SS1.p1.1 For  [0<\eta<1]  and  [\varepsilon_{\mathsf{prox}}\geqslant 0] , an
 [\varepsilon_{\mathsf{prox}}] -accurate proximal query at  [y]  returns a
measurable point  [\widetilde{x}_{y}]  satisfying





 [\lVert\widetilde{x}_{y}-\operatorname{prox}_{\eta V}(y)\rVert\leqslant\varepsilon_{\mathsf{prox}}\,.] 

(D.1)


@A4.SS1.p1.2 Given such a query and  [G\sim\mathsf{N}(0,I)] , define





 [\widehat{g}_{\eta,\varepsilon_{\mathsf{prox}}}(y;G)\coloneqq\nabla V(\widetilde{x}_{y}+\sqrt{\eta}\,G)\,.] 



@A4.SS1.p1.3 The approximate proximal variant of Algorithm 3.1 replaces every
occurrence of  [\widehat{g}_{\eta}]  by
 [\widehat{g}_{\eta,\varepsilon_{\mathsf{prox}}}] .


@A4.SS1.p2 

@A4.SS1.p2.1 The proof of Theorem 5.1 in the main text assumes exact
proximal queries. We now record the modifications needed when these calls
are replaced by the approximate proximal queries from
(D.1).


@A4.SS1.p3 

@A4.SS1.p3.1 For every queried center  [y] , let  [\widetilde{x}_{y}]  be the output of an
abstract  [\varepsilon_{\mathsf{prox}}] -accurate proximal oracle, and let
 [\widehat{K}^{\varepsilon_{\mathsf{prox}}}]  denote the resulting kernel.


@A4.Thmtheorem1 
Lemma D.1 (Stability under approximate proximal centers).

@A4.Thmtheorem1.p1 

@A4.Thmtheorem1.p1.1 If  [h^{2}\Lambda_{J}\leqslant 1] , then





 [W_{q}(\delta_{z}\widehat{K}^{\varepsilon_{\mathsf{prox}}}\,,\delta_{z}\widehat{K})\leqslant\mathcal{E}_{\rm prox}\coloneqq C\Lambda_{J}h\varepsilon_{\mathsf{prox}}\,,\qquad q\geqslant 1\,.] 

(D.2)


@A4.Thmtheorem1.p1.2 Consequently, for  [J\geqslant 2]  and  [h\leqslant c/(\kappa\,(q+\log J))] , and every phase-space law  [\widehat{\mu}] ,





 [\displaystyle W_{q,M_{\kappa}}^{2}(\widehat{\mu}(\widehat{K}^{\varepsilon_{\mathsf{prox}}})^{N},\Pi_{\eta})] 
 [\displaystyle\leqslant e^{-cNh/\kappa}\,W_{q,M_{\kappa}}^{2}(\widehat{\mu},\Pi_{\eta})+\frac{C\kappa q}{h}\,\mathcal{E}_{{\rm var},q}^{2}+\frac{C\kappa^{2}}{h^{2}}\,(\mathcal{E}_{{\rm bias}}^{2}+\mathcal{E}_{{\rm disc},q}^{2}+\mathcal{E}_{\rm prox}^{2})\,.] 

(D.3)




@A4.SS1.2 
Proof.

@A4.SS1.p4 

@A4.SS1.p4.1 For the same Gaussian  [G]  in the stochastic gradient,  [1] -smoothness and
(D.1) imply





 [\lVert\nabla V(\widetilde{x}_{y}+\sqrt{\eta}G)-\nabla V(x_{y}^{+}+\sqrt{\eta}G)\rVert\leqslant\varepsilon_{\mathsf{prox}}\,.] 



@A4.SS1.p4.2 The first stochastic gradient block therefore moves every Picard node by at most
 [h^{2}\Lambda_{J}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{prox}}] . The error in the second block
stochastic gradient is at most
 [\varepsilon_{\mathsf{prox}}+h^{2}\Lambda_{J}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{prox}}] . This yields position error at most
 [C\Lambda_{J}h^{2}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{prox}}]  and momentum error at most
 [Ch\varepsilon_{\mathsf{prox}}] , proving
(D.2).
Then, (D.3) by
applying the triangle inequality and Young’s inequality in the one-step recurrence
(5.2).
∎



@A4.SS1.p5 

@A4.SS1.p5.1 For the gradient-only implementation, set





 [\varepsilon_{\mathsf{prox}}\coloneqq c\mathfrak{L}_{q}^{-4}\sqrt{d}\,h^{3}\,.] 

(D.4)


@A4.SS1.p5.2 Since
 [\mathcal{E}_{\rm prox}\leqslant C\Lambda_{J}h\varepsilon_{\mathsf{prox}}] , we have





 [\frac{\kappa}{h}\,\mathcal{E}_{\rm prox}\leqslant C\kappa\Lambda_{J}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{prox}}\leqslant C\mathfrak{L}_{q}^{9}\kappa\sqrt{d+q}\,h^{3}\,.] 



@A4.SS1.p5.3 Thus (D.3) gives the same
conclusion as the exact proximal proof of Theorem 5.1.



@A4.SS2 

D.2 Gradient-only implementation


@A4.SS2.p1 

@A4.SS2.p1.1 The Picard HMC algorithm in Algorithm 3.1 is stated using exact
proximal queries, and Section 5.1 introduces its
 [\varepsilon_{\mathsf{prox}}] -accurate variant. This subsection implements every
such approximate query using only evaluations of the gradient.


@A4.SS2.p2 

@A4.SS2.p2.1 Fix a potential  [V]  satisfying
 [\kappa^{-1}I\preceq\nabla^{2}V\preceq I] , a smoothing variance
 [0<\eta\leqslant c_{0}<1] , and a center  [y] . Write  [x_{y}^{+}\coloneqq\operatorname{prox}_{\eta V}(y)] .


@A4.Thmtheorem2 
Algorithm D.2 (Gradient descent).

@A4.Thmtheorem2.p1 

@A4.Thmtheorem2.p1.1 Given  [y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}]  and  [\varepsilon_{\mathsf{prox}}>0] , set  [x^{(0)}\coloneqq y]  and
iterate





 [x^{(k+1)}\coloneqq y-\eta\nabla V(x^{(k)})] 



@A4.Thmtheorem2.p1.2 until





 [\lVert x^{(k+1)}-x^{(k)}\rVert\leqslant(1-\eta)\,\varepsilon_{\mathsf{prox}}\,.] 



@A4.Thmtheorem2.p1.3 Return  [\widetilde{x}_{y}\coloneqq x^{(k)}] .



@A4.Thmtheorem3 
Lemma D.3 (Certificate and pointwise work).

@A4.Thmtheorem3.p1 

@A4.Thmtheorem3.p1.1 The iteration in Algorithm D.2 stops after finitely many steps
and satisfies





 [\lVert\widetilde{x}_{y}-x_{y}^{+}\rVert\leqslant\varepsilon_{\mathsf{prox}}\,.] 

(D.5)


@A4.Thmtheorem3.p1.2 Its number  [N_{\mathsf{prox}}(y)]  of gradient evaluations obeys





 [N_{\mathsf{prox}}(y)\leqslant C_{c_{0}}\,\Bigl[1+\log\Bigl(1+\frac{\lVert\nabla V(y)\rVert}{\varepsilon_{\mathsf{prox}}}\Bigr)\Bigr]\,.] 

(D.6)




@A4.SS2.2 
Proof.

@A4.SS2.p3 

@A4.SS2.p3.1 The map  [T_{y}\coloneqq y-\eta\nabla V]  is an  [\eta] -contraction, and its
unique fixed point is  [x_{y}^{+}] . Therefore





 [\lVert x^{(k)}-x_{y}^{+}\rVert\leqslant\sum_{j=k}^{\infty}\,\lVert x^{(j+1)}-x^{(j)}\rVert\leqslant\frac{\lVert x^{(k+1)}-x^{(k)}\rVert}{1-\eta}\,,] 



@A4.SS2.p3.2 which proves the certificate is valid. Moreover,  [\lVert x^{(k+1)}-x^{(k)}\rVert\leqslant\eta^{k}\,\lVert x^{(1)}-x^{(0)}\rVert=\eta^{k+1}\,\lVert\nabla V(y)\rVert] .
Using  [\eta\leqslant c_{0}<1] 
gives (D.6).
∎



@A4.SS2.p4 

@A4.SS2.p4.1 The pointwise bound depends on the queried center. To obtain the expected
complexity claimed by the gradient-only sampler, we record that the centers
encountered along the realized run have controlled second moments.


@A4.Thmtheorem4 
Lemma D.4 (Expected work along the Picard HMC run).

@A4.Thmtheorem4.p1 

@A4.Thmtheorem4.p1.1 Under the parameter choices in
(5.12) and
(D.4), assume
also that  [h\mathfrak{L}_{q}\leqslant c/\kappa]  and  [\eta\leqslant c_{0}] , and implement every
 [\varepsilon_{\mathsf{prox}}] -accurate proximal query in
Algorithm 3.1 using
Algorithm D.2. Every argument  [Y]  supplied to this routine
obeys





 [\mathbb{E}\lVert\nabla V(Y)\rVert^{2}\leqslant C\kappa d\,\mathfrak{L}_{q}^{18}\,.] 

(D.7)


@A4.Thmtheorem4.p1.2 Consequently the expected total number of gradient evaluations is at most





 [\mathbb{E}N_{\nabla V}\leqslant C\,\frac{\kappa}{h}\,\mathfrak{L}_{q}^{3}\,.] 

(D.8)




@A4.SS2.3 
Proof.

@A4.SS2.p5 

@A4.SS2.p5.1 Let  [x_{\star}]  minimize  [V] . By
(D.3) with  [q=2]  and
Lemma 4.16,





 [\sup_{j\leqslant N}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\lVert X_{j}-x_{\star}\rVert^{2}+\lVert P_{j}\rVert^{2}\bigr]\leqslant C\kappa d\,\mathfrak{L}_{q}^{18}\,.] 



@A4.SS2.p5.2 This also controls every layer  [1]  node. Since
 [x_{x_{\star}}^{+}=x_{\star}] , non-expansiveness and
(D.5) give
 [\lVert\widetilde{x}_{Y}-x_{\star}\rVert\leqslant\lVert Y-x_{\star}\rVert+\varepsilon_{\mathsf{prox}}] ; hence  [1] -smoothness and Gaussian
second moments control the gradients used to form layer  [2] . The bound
 [\max_{i\in[J]}\sum_{j\in[J]}|\omega_{i,j}|\leqslant h^{2}\Lambda_{J}]  then controls
every layer  [2]  node. Finally,
 [\lVert\nabla V(Y)\rVert\leqslant\lVert Y-x_{\star}\rVert]  at either layer, proving
(D.7).


@A4.SS2.p6 

@A4.SS2.p6.1 By Lemma D.3 and Jensen’s inequality,





 [\mathbb{E}N_{\mathsf{prox}}(Y)\leqslant C\,\Bigl[1+\log\Bigl(1+\frac{(\mathbb{E}\lVert\nabla V(Y)\rVert^{2})^{1/2}}{\varepsilon_{\mathsf{prox}}}\Bigr)\Bigr]\leqslant C\mathfrak{L}_{q}\,.] 



@A4.SS2.p6.2 There are  [2J]  proximal and  [2J]  direct gradient calls per phase.
Using  [J\leqslant C\mathfrak{L}_{q}]  and
 [N\leqslant C\kappa\mathfrak{L}_{q}/h]  proves
(D.8).
∎



@A4.SS2.4 
Proof of Theorem 5.1.

@A4.SS2.p7 

@A4.SS2.p7.1 Replace every proximal query in Algorithm 3.1 by
Algorithm D.2 at the tolerance
(D.4).
The certificate in Lemma D.3 makes every call a valid
 [\varepsilon_{\mathsf{prox}}] -accurate proximal query. Then, Section D.1 gives the
claimed  [W_{q}]  bound, and the query bound follows from
Lemma D.4 after substituting the step size
chosen in the main text proof.
∎





@A5 

Appendix E Extension to arbitrary Picard depth


@A5.p1 

@A5.p1.1 The algorithm used in the main results takes Picard depth  [2] . In this appendix, we record the discretization error of Picard HMC using the exact smoothed gradient, at arbitrary depth.


@A5.p2 

@A5.p2.1 Retain the Chebyshev–Lobatto nodes and coefficients from
(3.6); their properties are summarized in
Appendix B. Let  [g_{\eta}\coloneqq\nabla V_{\eta}] , let
 [\Pi_{\eta}\coloneqq\pi_{\eta}\otimes\mathsf{N}(0,I)] , set
 [\Omega\coloneqq(\omega_{i,j})_{i,j=1}^{J}] . Recall that





 [\max_{i\in[J]}\sum_{j\in[J]}\lvert\omega_{i,j}\rvert\leqslant h^{2}\Lambda_{J}\,.] 




@A5.Thmtheorem1 
Algorithm E.1 (Depth- [K]  exact Picard HMC phase).

@A5.Thmtheorem1.p1 

@A5.Thmtheorem1.p1.1 Given  [(X_{\rm init},P_{\rm init})] , draw independent
 [\zeta_{0},\zeta_{1}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,I)] , set
 [a_{h}\coloneqq e^{-h/2}] ,  [\sigma_{h}\coloneqq(1-e^{-h})^{1/2}] , and define





 [\displaystyle P_{0}] 
 [\displaystyle\coloneqq a_{h}P_{\rm init}+\sigma_{h}\zeta_{0}\,,] 
 [\displaystyle X_{t_{i}}^{[0]}] 
 [\displaystyle\coloneqq X_{\rm init}+t_{i}P_{0}\,,] 




 [\displaystyle X_{t_{i}}^{[\ell+1]}] 
 [\displaystyle\coloneqq X_{\rm init}+t_{i}P_{0}-\sum_{j\in[J]}\omega_{i,j}g_{\eta}(X_{t_{j}}^{[\ell]})\,,] 

 [\displaystyle 0\leqslant\ell<K-1\,.] 

(E.1)


@A5.Thmtheorem1.p1.2 Set





 [\displaystyle P_{h}^{[K]}] 
 [\displaystyle\coloneqq P_{0}-\sum_{j\in[J]}\omega_{j}g_{\eta}(X_{t_{j}}^{[K-1]})\,,] 
 [\displaystyle X_{h}^{[K]}] 
 [\displaystyle\coloneqq X_{\rm init}+hP_{0}-\sum_{j\in[J]}\omega_{J,j}g_{\eta}(X_{t_{j}}^{[K-1]})\,,] 



@A5.Thmtheorem1.p1.3 and write the resulting next-phase initialization as





 [\displaystyle X_{\rm init}^{+}] 
 [\displaystyle\coloneqq X_{h}^{[K]}\,,] 
 [\displaystyle P_{\rm init}^{+}] 
 [\displaystyle\coloneqq a_{h}P_{h}^{[K]}+\sigma_{h}\zeta_{1}\,.] 



@A5.Thmtheorem1.p1.4 For  [z=(X_{\rm init},P_{\rm init})]  and
 [\zeta=(\zeta_{0},\zeta_{1})] , write
 [\Phi_{z}^{[J,K]}(\zeta)\coloneqq(X_{\rm init}^{+},P_{\rm init}^{+})]  and
 [K_{\eta,h}^{[J,K]}]  for its kernel.



@A5.Thmtheorem2 
Theorem E.2 (Depth- [K]  Picard HMC).

@A5.Thmtheorem2.p1 

@A5.Thmtheorem2.p1.1 Suppose  [\kappa\geqslant 1] ,  [0<\eta\leqslant 1] ,  [V\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})] , and
 [\kappa^{-1}I\preceq\nabla^{2}V\preceq I] . There are
universal  [c,C>0]  such that, if





 [h^{2}\Lambda_{J}\leqslant\frac{1}{4}\,,\qquad h\Lambda_{J}\leqslant\frac{c}{\kappa}\,,] 

(E.2)


@A5.Thmtheorem2.p1.2 then, for every integer  [K\geqslant 1] , every  [q\geqslant 2] , every
 [z,z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}] , and every  [\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}] ,





 [\displaystyle W_{q,M_{\kappa}}\bigl(\delta_{z}K_{\eta,h}^{[J,K]}\,,\delta_{z^{\prime}}K_{\eta,h}^{[J,K]}\bigr)] 
 [\displaystyle\leqslant(1-ch/\kappa)\,\lVert z-z^{\prime}\rVert_{M_{\kappa}}\,,] 

(E.3)



 [\displaystyle\lVert\{\Phi_{z}^{[J,K]}(\zeta)-z\}-\{\Phi_{z^{\prime}}^{[J,K]}(\zeta)-z^{\prime}\}\rVert_{M_{\kappa}}] 
 [\displaystyle\leqslant Ch\,\lVert z-z^{\prime}\rVert_{M_{\kappa}}\,,] 

(E.4)



 [\displaystyle W_{q,M_{\kappa}}\bigl(\Pi_{\eta}K_{\eta,h}^{[J,K]}\,,\Pi_{\eta}\bigr)] 
 [\displaystyle\leqslant C\Lambda_{J}\sqrt{d+q}\,\Bigl\{B_{J,q}h\,\Bigl(\frac{h}{\sqrt{\eta}}\Bigr)^{J}+h^{3}\,(h^{2}\Lambda_{J})^{K-1}\Bigr\}\,,] 

(E.5)


@A5.Thmtheorem2.p1.3 where  [B_{J,q}\coloneqq C_{0}^{J+1}q^{2J}J!] , and  [C_{0}]  is universal.
In particular,  [K=2]  recovers Proposition 5.9.



@A5.2 
Proof.

@A5.p3 

@A5.p3.1 Contraction and coupling.
As in the proof of Proposition 4.7, couple inputs
 [z=(x_{\rm init},p_{\rm init})]  and
 [z^{\prime}=(x^{\prime}_{\rm init},p^{\prime}_{\rm init})]  using the same Gaussian variables, and
write  [\Delta_{0}\coloneqq z-z^{\prime}=(\Delta x,\Delta p)] ,  [\Delta_{1}\coloneqq\Phi_{z}^{[J,K]}(\zeta)-\Phi_{z^{\prime}}^{[J,K]}(\zeta)] .
Then,





 [\displaystyle\Delta X_{t_{i}}^{[0]}] 
 [\displaystyle=\Delta x+a_{h}t_{i}\Delta p\,,\qquad\Delta X_{t_{i}}^{[\ell+1]}=\Delta X_{t_{i}}^{[0]}-\sum_{j\in[J]}\omega_{i,j}H_{j}^{[\ell]}\Delta X_{t_{j}}^{[\ell]}\,,] 



@A5.p3.2 where





 [H_{j}^{[\ell]}\coloneqq H\bigl(X_{t_{j}}^{[\ell]},X_{t_{j}}^{\prime[\ell]}\bigr)\,,\qquad(2\kappa)^{-1}I\preceq H_{j}^{[\ell]}\preceq I\,.] 



@A5.p3.3 Since  [\max_{i\in[J]}\sum_{j\in[J]}\lvert\omega_{i,j}\rvert\leqslant h^{2}\Lambda_{J}\leqslant 1/4] ,
induction in  [\ell]  gives





 [\max_{j\in[J]}\,\lVert\Delta X_{t_{j}}^{[K-1]}-\Delta X_{t_{j}}^{[0]}\rVert\leqslant C\Lambda_{J}h^{2}\,\lVert\Delta_{0}\rVert\,.] 



@A5.p3.4 Now set





 [H_{j}\coloneqq H\bigl(X_{t_{j}}^{[K-1]},X_{t_{j}}^{\prime[K-1]}\bigr)\,,\qquad H\coloneqq h^{-1}\sum_{j\in[J]}\omega_{j}H_{j}\,.] 



@A5.p3.5 The endpoint calculation in
Lemma 4.6 therefore applies unchanged and
yields





 [\Delta_{1}=(I+hA_{H})\Delta_{0}+\mathcal{R}_{h}\Delta_{0}\,,\qquad\lVert\mathcal{R}_{h}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant C\Lambda_{J}h^{2}\,.] 



@A5.p3.6 The matrix inequality in the proof of
Proposition 4.7, followed by
 [h\Lambda_{J}\leqslant c/\kappa] , proves
(E.3).
Moreover, subtracting  [\Delta_{0}]  from the expansion and using
 [\lVert A_{H}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant C]  gives





 [\lVert\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{0}\rVert_{M_{\kappa}}\leqslant C\,(h+\Lambda_{J}h^{2})\,\lVert\Delta_{0}\rVert_{M_{\kappa}}\leqslant Ch\,\lVert\Delta_{0}\rVert_{M_{\kappa}}\,,] 



@A5.p3.7 which is (E.4).


@A5.p4 

@A5.p4.1 Discretization error.
Start the phase from  [\Pi_{\eta}] .
The first OU half-refresh preserves this law. Let  [X_{t}] ,
 [0\leqslant t\leqslant h] , be the position along the exact Hamiltonian trajectory,
and put  [g(t)\coloneqq g_{\eta}(X_{t})] .
The interpolation estimate already proved in
(5.11) gives





 [\mathcal{E}_{J,q}\coloneqq\sup_{0\leqslant t\leqslant h}\,\lVert g(t)-\mathcal{I}_{J,h}g(t)\rVert_{L^{q}}\leqslant B_{J,q}\sqrt{d+q}\,\Bigl(\frac{h}{\sqrt{\eta}}\Bigr)^{J}\,.] 

(E.6)



@A5.p5 

@A5.p5.1 Let  [\mathsf{X}\coloneqq(X_{t_{j}})_{j\in[J]}]  and
 [\mathsf{X}^{[0]}\coloneqq(X_{t_{j}}^{[0]})_{j\in[J]}] , and use the norm given by
the maximum over nodes of the  [L^{q}] -norm. The exact Volterra equation
and polynomial interpolation give





 [\mathsf{X}=\mathsf{X}^{[0]}-\Omega g_{\eta}(\mathsf{X})+\varrho\,,\qquad\lVert\varrho\rVert\leqslant\frac{h^{2}}{2}\,\mathcal{E}_{J,q}\,.] 

(E.7)


@A5.p5.2 The map  [\mathcal{T}:\mathsf{y}\mapsto\mathsf{X}^{[0]}-\Omega g_{\eta}(\mathsf{y})]  has Lipschitz constant at most  [h^{2}\Lambda_{J}] , because  [g_{\eta}]  is
 [1] -Lipschitz. Let  [\overline{\mathsf{X}}]  be its fixed point. From
(E.7),





 [\lVert\overline{\mathsf{X}}-\mathsf{X}\rVert\leqslant\frac{h^{2}}{2\,(1-h^{2}\Lambda_{J})}\,\mathcal{E}_{J,q}\,.] 

(E.8)


@A5.p5.3 The proof of Proposition 5.9 gives
 [\sup_{0\leqslant t\leqslant h}\,\lVert g(t)\rVert_{L^{q}}\leqslant C\sqrt{d+q}] . Combining this with
(E.8) and the fixed-point equation yields





 [\lVert\mathsf{X}^{[0]}-\overline{\mathsf{X}}\rVert\leqslant C\Lambda_{J}h^{2}\sqrt{d+q}+C\Lambda_{J}h^{2}\mathcal{E}_{J,q}\,.] 



@A5.p5.4 The recursion (E.1) is precisely the iteration of
 [\mathcal{T}] , starting from  [\mathsf{X}^{[0]}] . Hence





 [\lVert\mathsf{X}^{[K-1]}-\overline{\mathsf{X}}\rVert\leqslant C\,(h^{2}\Lambda_{J})^{K-1}\,\Lambda_{J}h^{2}\,\{\sqrt{d+q}+\mathcal{E}_{J,q}\}\,.] 

(E.9)



@A5.p6 

@A5.p6.1 The exact Hamiltonian endpoint satisfies





 [\displaystyle X_{h}] 
 [\displaystyle=X_{\rm init}+hP_{0}-\int_{0}^{h}(h-t)g(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\,,\qquad P_{h}=P_{0}-\int_{0}^{h}g(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\,.] 



@A5.p6.2 The quadrature rules integrate  [\mathcal{I}_{J,h}g]  exactly. Use
(E.8)–(E.9),
 [1] -Lipschitzness of  [g_{\eta}] , and the coefficient bounds in
(B.2). The momentum estimate dominates and
gives





 [\lVert(X_{\rm init}^{+},P_{\rm init}^{+})-(X_{h},P_{h}^{+})\rVert_{L^{q},M_{\kappa}}\leqslant C\Lambda_{J}\,\bigl\{h\mathcal{E}_{J,q}+h^{3}\,(h^{2}\Lambda_{J})^{K-1}\sqrt{d+q}\bigr\}\,.] 

(E.10)


@A5.p6.3 Here  [P_{h}^{+}]  includes the common final OU half-refresh. The terms in
(E.9) containing  [\mathcal{E}_{J,q}]  are
absorbed into the first term because  [h^{2}\Lambda_{J}\leqslant 1/4]  and
 [h\leqslant 1] .
Substituting
(E.6) into
(E.10) proves
(E.5).
∎

Instructions for reporting errors
    
We are continuing to improve HTML versions of papers, and your feedback helps enhance accessibility and mobile
      support. To report errors in the HTML that will help us improve conversion and rendering, choose any of the
      methods listed below:
    
      Click the "Report Issue" (
            
          ) button, located in the page header.
    
    
Tip: You can select the relevant text first, to include it in your report.
    
Our team has already identified the following issues. We appreciate your time reviewing and reporting rendering errors we
      may not have found yet. Your efforts will help us improve the HTML versions for all readers, because disability
      should not be a barrier to accessing research. Thank you for your continued support in championing open access for
      all.
    
Have a free development cycle? Help support accessibility at arXiv! Our collaborators at LaTeXML maintain a list of packages that need conversion, and welcome developer contributions.
